A populated rolling upgrade retains previous claim/heartbeat DTOs, runtime readers and authority gates for existing legacy grants; version/capability checks at registration, claim and adoption prevent assigning a new-format recovery to an incompatible worker/runtime pair. Activate bounded-history routes and the required completion acknowledgement only after compatible writers and eligible peers are established and legacy authority is affirmatively drained. Timeout or lease replacement is not drain evidence. If these conditions cannot preserve N−1 and active-execution compatibility, activation is refused pending an owner-approved migration design.

#### Runtime input and output

Replace `Runtime.recovery(attempt_id, grants: tuple[GrantBinding,...])` with explicit bounded operations:

- `grant_history_status(attempt_id, current_grant, snapshot) -> GrantHistoryProgress`.
- `accept_grant_history(attempt_id, current_grant, page: GrantHistoryPage) -> GrantHistoryProgress`.
- `recovery(attempt_id, current_grant, request: RecoveryRead) -> RecoveryProgress | RecoveryPage`.

`GrantHistoryProgress` is `{snapshot, accepted: GrantHistoryPosition, complete}`. Progress denotes durably accepted contiguous input only. A status call reconstructs it from durable verified records after process loss. The runtime authenticates the caller through its existing control channel and binds current_grant to the peer, attempt, original domain and known incarnation; a descriptor or position cannot establish that binding. Old-origin `record_dispatcher_quiescence` keeps its narrowly scoped closure-only authentication exception and cannot upload a new history or dispatch work.

The runtime persists immutable grant records once, with their verified prefix digests. Its compact registry head holds only positions/identities and evidence descriptors. It does not deserialize/rewrite all history under the local registry lock. Use per-attempt durable indexed records with bounded atomic page/checkpoint commit; interrupted writes are either absent or reconstructibly committed. The implementation may choose the storage mechanism under #195/#196, but must prove bounded per-call I/O and crash consistency. This explicitly revises a single-JSON registry layout where unbounded grant/closure arrays would defeat finite calls. Retained authoritative history/evidence survives release and retirement; disposal of transfers cannot delete it or reopen a fence.

A 512-byte encoded allowance per grant cannot contain a legal immutable GrantBinding: its seven printable identifiers alone can require 3,584 escaped bytes. The retained-record limits are: compact head at most 64 KiB and retained encoded binding/closure/index records at most 16 KiB per grant, plus the VcsRef contract's existing separately retained result-file allowance. These limits are approved under issue #226. DispatcherClosure identifier/evidence-ref fields use the existing 256-printable-character Identifier; timestamps serialize as fixed UTC RFC3339 with six fractional digits. Full canonical binding is below 4 KiB and closure below 5 KiB; the balance covers the finite index/prefix metadata. Filesystem/page allocation overhead is measured separately, not represented as encoded data. Compact head and all authoritative history records remain after release; disposable scan/page files are removed only when acknowledged progress remains reconstructible. Retirement and full-disk fallback never gather/copy that history; they update only the compact fenced head. Failed writes acknowledge nothing, and a missing/corrupt acceleration index is rebuilt in bounded recoverable steps rather than a whole-history scan inside one call. No new frame, lock or operation timeout is introduced.

Explicit full-disk amendment: a first attachment/adoption requiring a new immutable peer/grant record returns retryable journal_unavailable until that record can be persisted. It is not accepted into an unbounded in-memory set. A reconnect to an already durable binding remains observational under existing authentication and the full-disk fence. Internal stop escalation and already-authorized stop/observation paths remain independent of history writes; refusal establishes no new authority or closure. Existing durable identities and evidence are never dropped, and the compact-head retry references their unchanged records. This narrows the former blanket statement that attach/adopt remains accepted during ENOSPC/EDQUOT; the approved issue #226 contract requires it.

`RecoveryRead` is a tagged union: `{kind: resume}`; `{kind: begin, snapshot, expected_previous_generation}`; or `{kind: acknowledge, generation, evidence_snapshot, end, dispositions}`. Generation is an existing bounded nonnegative Count; the first pass expects zero. Begin compare-and-sets the last fully delivered generation to its successor; identical response-loss replay returns that pass, while a conflicting begin or a begin with an unacknowledged prior output is refused. Resume reads the retained current pass, never silently starts another. The runtime owns the durable scan position; callers cannot skip inspected grants. One pinned pass inspects the finite accepted snapshot and yields bounded RecoveryProgress `{generation, snapshot, scanned, phase}`. Before input completion it can only report phase awaiting_input; stop and direct closure recording remain available.

At scan completion, freeze `ClosureSnapshot {version, namespace_id, attempt_id, execution_domain_id, generation, grant_snapshot, count, digest}` over the finite prefix of the runtime's append-only closure log. Closure sequence order is committed append order; exact duplicates are no-ops and conflicts refuse. Its hash chain uses the distinct seed `kora.dispatcher-closure.v1`, the same scoped identity and canonical complete DispatcherClosure records. Snapshot count fixes its high-water mark. Closure append during output does not mutate it and is delivered by a later pass. `RecoveryPage {generation, evidence_snapshot, start, closed_dispatchers, end, complete, observation}` carries a bounded closure page; start/end carry sequence/count/prefix_digest. Its `observation: RecoveryEvidence | null` is null on nonfinal pages and is the frozen logical observation on the final page, including an empty final page. The observation replaces its whole-history closed_dispatchers tuple with this same ClosureSnapshot descriptor. The wire control frame carries compact observation metadata and authenticated length/digest references; the existing separate Handle.data/publication sections below reconstruct the logical field. No partially reconstructed RecoveryPage is returned. Final acknowledgement requires receiving the complete observation; intermediate pages do not retransmit its content sections. The first page can start at the processed-delivery prefix preserved from the prior pass, verified against the new frozen prefix. All grants still must be scanned in a later pass to revisit previously open grants. Count/digest are checked even for an empty final page.

The existing logical RecoveryEvidence keeps its handle, termination, normal-completion and PublicationOutcome types; only the whole-history closed_dispatchers tuple becomes the ClosureSnapshot descriptor. The completed pass freezes that finite observation alongside the evidence descriptor, retaining its capture timestamp across replay. Compact observation metadata is at most 64 KiB within the 256 KiB page envelope. `Handle.data` uses a separate authenticated content section of at most its existing 1 MiB; publication content keeps the VcsRef contract's separate at-most-1-MiB section. Each uses length/digest framing, at most 64 KiB byte frames and the unchanged 1 MiB control-frame limit. Reconstruct the typed observation only after all required sections validate; missing, extra, mismatched or truncated sections yield no partial typed observation. A VcsRef stays a VcsRef and transport descriptors do not become substitute domain models. Only one frozen observation and one bounded page transaction are retained; their content limits apply separately from authoritative history. A failed observation transfer replays the saved sections under the existing operation deadline, without rescanning or creating an effect. The original immutable publication result retains its independent ownership.

The runtime saves a separate processed-delivery position and at most one pending output page boundary. Acknowledge covers the exact pending generation, frozen snapshot and end; its bounded dispositions cover every pending item exactly once by closure sequence and canonical item digest. `accepted` means a successful authenticated API heartbeat accepted that exact submitted item. `refused` includes only the fixed safe code evidence_unbound or evidence_conflict from an authenticated API response naming that exact item. Both API refusals must identify its request index and dispatcher/epoch tuple. A whole-request refusal accepts nothing; a successful reduced heartbeat accepts only its submitted subset. Ambiguous identification, API008, credential refusal, timeout and transport failure produce neither disposition. The runtime authenticates this ACK through the existing current-worker control context. It stores refusal code and item digest alongside the immutable closure record, without changing its payload or hash-chain input. A refusal cannot be overwritten by reduced success for a different subset.

Persist the complete bounded page dispositions and processed-delivery position atomically before offering the next page. Resume after runtime/worker restart replays any pending page or the committed disposition checkpoint; response loss after API commit safely repeats the same evidence heartbeat. Response loss after the runtime ACK returns the same committed delivery position. The final page also needs ACK, returning phase delivered; an empty final page requires an empty disposition list after the complete observation arrives. Only then may begin start another bounded inspection pass. New authenticated worker grants resume the old pass without discarding progress; before effects they separately ingest and inspect the latest grant prefix. No per-page session table or unbounded collection of generations is retained. Refused records remain durable and unresolved: advancing delivery permits later pages and inspection only, never closure, replacement, reservation release or any effect. A new pass can revisit open grants; it does not blindly resubmit the same permanently refused immutable record. Changed authoritative state or an explicit reconciliation decision may cause bounded re-evaluation, preserving the original evidence and refusal. There is no immediate retry/rescan loop.

Recovery begins its pinned inspection pass only once its input snapshot is fully verified. Independently authenticated individual closures may still be recorded/relayed before that; they do not complete the pass. A completed scan means every grant in that fixed snapshot was inspected, not that all grants are closed. Partial history and an empty completed closure stream cannot certify total absence, all-dispatcher closure, publication readiness or replacement readiness. Runtime cancellation, stop escalation and direct closure recording remain available independently of transfer completion.

#### Replay, append and authority

The worker keeps two independent receiver-confirmed positions: runtime-accepted grant input and durably processed closure delivery. It never discards a grant page before runtime durable acknowledgement, or a closure page before the runtime durably records its exact API-derived dispositions. Response loss replays the same snapshot/start/end and bytes. Identical replay returns durable progress and produces no grant, event duplication or publication effect. A conflicting page is a content-free conflict; gaps cannot be skipped. Connection loss closes sockets/tasks/buffers, and resume starts from receiver status. A restarted worker obtains current authority and reuses the authenticated verified prefix; a restarted runtime reconstructs durable accepted progress. If disposable state was removed, replaying from the last retained verified boundary is safe.

Each claimed attempt owns at most one input page and one pending output page; total active buffers are therefore bounded by configured worker capacity, not retained-history length. Transfer admission is fair among claimed attempts. A parked or failed attempt releases sockets, decoders and active transfer slots while retaining only its bounded page/disposition state and durable runtime checkpoint, allowing another attempt to progress. Shared HTTP-client admission reserves ordinary heartbeat/stop capacity before admitting optional history reads; a deployment pool too small to provide both serializes/preempts history reads instead of starving liveness. Response byte bounds apply while reading, before JSON decoding.

The existing single persistent runtime control connection is preserved. A history/recovery transfer is cancellable and has lower priority than authenticated stop/observation control. The worker cancels the in-flight transfer and closes its connection when necessary, releases its buffers and reconnects through the existing binding/deadline rules for priority control; it never waits for a whole multi-section result or scan to finish first. The supervisor checkpoints only confirmed progress and its internal stop escalation runs independently of connection I/O and history storage. Ordinary API heartbeats do not await runtime recovery or fresh optional counters: they may omit unavailable observations, never relabel old observations as fresh. Existing connection caps, operation deadlines and absolute stop thresholds remain unchanged.

On API008 the worker closes its effect gate immediately. After an explicitly identified evidence_unbound/evidence_conflict it records that item as refused, removes only it from the next submitted subset and follows the existing heartbeat-without-bad-evidence rule. The page can advance only after all its exact items have API-derived dispositions. Each bounded isolation attempt yields/checks the existing deadline, so up to 128 bad items cannot monopolize the worker or produce an immediate retry loop. Ordinary heartbeat, cancellation, event relay and other attempts continue independently. Refused evidence remains unresolved and never satisfies an authority barrier.

Concurrent grant append does not mutate an in-flight snapshot. The old prefix remains readable to an authorized current holder. Before an effect, the authoritative current grant tip must equal the completed snapshot. A newer tip requires verifying its extension; it cannot be hidden by completion of an old snapshot. Closure appends do not change grant identity and are delivered through the independent evidence sequence. Neither continuous append nor repeated deadlines promises completion under permanent interference; every successful bounded call commits finite progress when work is available.

Heartbeat request gains optional `grant_history_complete: GrantHistorySnapshot`; response gains `grant_history_verified: GrantHistorySnapshot | null`. The field is the trusted worker's acknowledgement that the runtime verified the full input. Under the same run lock/transaction as evidence validation and permitted_actions recomputation, the engine compares it with the current committed tip and records a current-grant-scoped acknowledgement. Shape failure is API003, a well-formed nonmatching tuple API002/grant_history_conflict, and stale authority API008. Any failure commits neither this acknowledgement nor submitted closure evidence. Identical replay writes nothing. The response echoes only a currently matching recorded descriptor; it is not a termination receipt. The snapshot remains supplied until verification, even if every earlier dispatcher is already closed. Recovered effect/report_result/current-output authority additionally requires that acknowledgement, every authoritative earlier grant closed, publication reconciliation, result authority and unchanged deadline/epoch checks. Stop, retire, closure relay, cancellation/loss and operator-attestation paths retain their existing independent evidence conditions; a transport failure cannot disable them. The runtime locally also requires complete verified input before recovered effect dispatch. No client-reported page count or complete flag bypasses those checks. A new grant clears the acknowledgement; closure cannot reopen it into effect authority.

The worker retains the post-acquisition fresh PublicationExpectation exact echo and local dispatch gate before each finalize/retry. Finalization, new publication, normal result acceptance, replacement/reservation release and existing operator-attestation rules keep their current separate conditions. A current tip lookup plus indexed unresolved-grant predicate replaces full-history loading for the closure decision. Terminal reports still include their own grant's closure; earlier closure evidence is relayed monotonically and omitted after acknowledgement. Recovery transfer expiration or failure is never evidence of stopped execution.

#### Ownership, cleanup and diagnostics

Required durable grant/closure records and their prefix indexes are retained with attempt recovery metadata; they are not leaked transfer buffers. One receiving progress head exists per attempt/domain/current grant; replacing it cannot erase retained verified records. No per-request snapshot session, open DB cursor, historical list cache or in-memory task per grant is retained. Each call owns one bounded page, its network/DB resources and temporary serialization state, and releases them on success, malformed input, stale authority, error, cancellation, deadline, disconnect and shutdown. Registry loss is unknown and never reconstructed as empty history.

Diagnostics use content-free event codes, component-owned context, counts, bytes and progress state. No bearer token, full cursor/descriptor, grant arrays, ProcessIdentity, payload/result/principal values or raw validation exception is logged. Operational retry diagnostics are bounded/coalesced under existing observability policy; durable closure audit events retain their existing single owner and idempotency.

#### Protocol traces and future evidence

Positive trace: 1,000 grants require at least eight 128-row pages. API serves a fixed committed prefix; runtime durably accepts two pages; the second acknowledgement is lost. Replaying page two returns the same position. A later call reaches its deadline after confirmed progress, then the worker restarts and claims the same attempt under a new grant. It obtains a fresh descriptor, compares/reuses the durable prefix, transfers the remaining extension and verifies final count/digest. Closure pages similarly replay until exact API-derived dispositions are durably recorded; accepted entries need no repeat, and refused entries remain preserved without blocking later pages. Only the completed latest snapshot and authoritative closure plus existing gates allow any recovered effect. No extra claim is made for continuation and no effect is repeated to reconstruct a response.

Negative traces cover forged/cross-attempt/domain positions; wrong worker/incarnation; revoked token/stale epoch; altered count/digest/order; skipped/duplicate/conflicting input; source/runtime response loss on either side of durable commit; begin, scan-completion and final-page-ACK response loss; worker death between API commit and runtime ACK; whole-request refusal followed by reduced success and exact accepted/refused item mapping; refused first/middle/last items followed by valid pages and a later inspection pass; wrong generation/item digest or ambiguous refusal; empty closure output with a complete frozen observation, including no live runner and after release; observation-section response loss and exact replay; a previously live worker exiting before the next pass; new grant immediately before final authority comparison; closure append during reading; timeout during local registry write; missing index reconstruction; registry loss; stop/cancel while transfer is incomplete or a publication echo is in flight; and terminal own-grant evidence without replaying all old closures. Every refusal preserves retained history and barriers.

Future named cases include `test_grant_history_transfer_resumes_after_deadline`, `test_grant_history_snapshot_rejects_invalid_continuation`, `test_grant_history_partial_transfer_cannot_authorize_effects`, `test_grant_history_append_preserves_prefix`, `test_grant_history_evidence_replay_is_monotonic`, `test_grant_history_registry_commit_is_reconstructible` and `test_grant_history_transfer_releases_resources`. DTO/codec checks belong to #156 and are implemented under #262; engine/metadata and API integration to work items 9/10/11; local registry/protocol recovery to #195/#196/#197; worker bridge to work item 17. #199 owns runner harness hosting, not the worker. Implementing issues for the unassigned work-item-9/10/11/17 portions must exist before implementation/activation is claimed; their status is pending and establishes no consumer coverage. Every DB integration runs SQLite and PostgreSQL; process/registry coverage runs Linux and macOS. Runtime models, API, worker and runtime behavior remain pending implementation.

Measurements must cover 1, 128, 129 and 1,000 grants, including mostly closed histories, near-byte-cap rows and histories larger than one operation deadline; query counts/indexed work, lock hold, scheduler fairness, page/serialization peak bytes, durable growth separately from transient growth, and repeated success/refusal/replay/cancellation/deadline/restart/shutdown cycles. Preregister samples and justified gates in the implementing issue, preserving existing lifecycle gates and the ten-minute CI budget. No measurement is established by this contract.

Include the combined maximum Handle.data section, maximum publication section and near-cap closure page, with disconnect/restart between content sections; measure transient buffers, frozen observation storage and cleanup separately. Full-disk first-attach refusal and reconnect to durable bindings must remain bounded under repeated distinct callers, with stop escalation independent.

`test_grant_history_diagnostics_are_content_free` belongs to each API/worker/runtime activation: success, refusal, replay, cancellation and DEBUG failure paths record only safe codes/counts/bytes/progress, exclude credentials/descriptors/process identities/evidence bodies/raw exceptions, and preserve heartbeat and stop progress with a blocked diagnostic sink. Audit closure events retain their existing owner and replay idempotency.

`test_grant_history_read_reauthorizes_every_page` covers missing/revoked/expired/cookie-only credentials (API009), personal principals/wrong workers (API005), indistinguishable foreign/absent attempts (API001), stale grants before snapshot comparison (API008), and grant_history_conflict only after authorization. Repeat on continuation/replay after revocation and grant replacement on both databases. Atomic heartbeat tests submit invalid snapshot plus valid closure and valid snapshot plus invalid closure and prove neither half commits; a new-grant race invalidates the previous verified acknowledgement.

A capacity-two worker trace parks one attempt on refused evidence while the other transfers, acknowledges and reports. Explicit barriers hold history HTTP responses and runtime content sections while heartbeats, cancellation, local gate closure, event relay and absolute stop escalation proceed. The lost path closes its gate and starts retirement while transfer remains blocked; retirement does not wait for transfer completion, and reporting `lost` still requires its existing evidence. Repeated interrupted input/output/disposition cycles measure buffers, sockets, tasks, timers, descriptors and spools in one long-lived worker. Slow response consumers must hold no DB session; truncated/oversized/cancelled responses release resources and preserve checkpoints.

Migration cases on both databases interrupt immediately before/after each batch commit, append during backfill and final activation, retain a legacy writer during the compatibility phase, and prove unrelated heartbeat/scheduling progress during catch-up. `test_grant_history_activation_preserves_legacy_peers` covers an old worker and supervisor after API upgrade, active recovery and attempted premature activation. They verify bounded query plans, no whole-history scan under the authority lock and no route activation while a legacy grant writer remains.

### Local runtime (issue #123)

Everything here is cooperative on `local` (ADR-0016): same-user code can read `KORA_ATTEMPT_TOKEN`, edit the registry and journal, and kill a worker to produce real exit evidence. The design follows the [local runner adoption probe](../research/local-runner-adoption-probe-2026-10-19.md), which ran on macOS only, and the [mechanisms probe of 2026-10-22](../research/local-runtime-mechanisms-probe-2026-10-22.md), which checked on GitHub's Linux and macOS runners the mechanisms its record lists; the runner work item verifies the rest on Linux and macOS CI, among them peer attestation on an accepted listening socket and from the connecting side, the pid read from a pidfd's `fdinfo`, the process start time on macOS, the exit status carried in a `NOTE_EXITSTATUS` event and every service-manager rule below.

**One detached `kora-runner` per attempt, found through a registry per execution domain.**

- **State root.** The runtime configuration field `state_root` defaults to `$XDG_STATE_HOME/kora/local` on Linux when that variable is an absolute path (otherwise `~/.local/state/kora/local`) and `~/Library/Application Support/kora/local` on macOS. A configured or default root is printable text of at most 4,052 characters, so `<state_root>/workspaces/<name>` always fits a 4,096-character path (issue #323). It is opened `O_DIRECTORY|O_NOFOLLOW` and checked with `fstat` (owner the user, mode 0700); everything beneath it is opened relative to that descriptor. A symlink anywhere on the path, a wrong owner or mode, an ancestor owned by neither the user nor root, a missing root or a network or user-space filesystem is refused with the non-retryable `state_root_unsafe`, never created or repaired. Every filesystem, text or home-directory failure to resolve or open the root maps to that one code, transient ones such as `EMFILE` or `EIO` included: the factory runs once when the worker starts, the refusal stops the worker before it claims work, and the error carries a fixed message, empty details and no chained original error, so no path reaches it (issue #323). The filesystem type is `fstatfs` `f_type` on Linux and `f_fstypename` on macOS, refused from a fixed table (issue #312): on Linux nfs, smb, cifs, smb2, fuse (every FUSE subtype shares its one magic), coda, afs, 9p, ceph, ncp, lustre, gfs2, ocfs2, gpfs, nfsd, orangefs and beegfs; on macOS nfs, smbfs, afpfs, webdav, cifs, ftp, macfuse, osxfuse and fusefs. The socket directory below is opened through the same check.
- **`domain.json`** is `{format: "local/1", execution_domain_id, host_id, boots: [{boot_id, pid_namespace}]}`, canonical JSON of at most 1 MiB; `boots` is the state root's boot log. The async factory `LocalRuntime.open` (issue #323) reads or creates it through `kora.runner.registry.open_domain` under the root-level lock file `boot.lock` (taken with the registry's lock rule below), before the runtime object exists, so the runtime's constructor changes no state. The file is created only in an empty root, meaning one without a `registry` directory: it is written as a temporary file, synced and moved with a no-replace rename (`renameat2(RENAME_NOREPLACE)` on Linux, `renameatx_np(RENAME_EXCL)` on macOS), and only then is `registry` created, so a crash between them leaves an empty root; a creator that loses the rename reads the winner's file, and leftover `domain.json.tmp-*` files are removed under `boot.lock`. A new `boot_id` is appended, once, by atomic replacement; a new boot past 4,096 recorded boots is refused with the non-retryable `boot_log_full`. A changed `host_id`, a recorded `boot_id` with a different pid namespace, an own identity with a null `host_id`, `boot_id` or `pid_namespace`, and a `registry` directory without `domain.json`, and a failed read or write of the root's lineage files are the lineage error `lineage_unavailable`: retryable, never `None`.
- **Spawn.** `submit` starts the runner through the runner launch command injected into the runtime (issue #136). It double-forks into a new session, with stdin `/dev/null`, every descriptor closed except one readiness pipe, and a constructed minimal environment with no API token, database URL or worker configuration secret. The worker waits on the readiness pipe with a deadline and reaps only the launcher. In local conformance, the injected lower-layer `LocalLaunchAuthority` instead uses a parent-owned gated detached child, with final process/session identity and exact runtime binding recorded before activation and readiness. The [local conformance launch seam](conformance-lifetime.md#local-runtime-seam) governs reservation before `starting` or persistent-state effects, independent cleanup after pytest exit, managed runner/SDK child parentage and outer disposable-domain teardown. The serving double-fork path and runtime registry/retirement evidence remain unchanged.
- **The launch and readiness** (issue #313). The runner launch command is a keyword argument of `LocalRuntime.open`; the production default is `(sys.executable, "-I", "-m", "kora.plugins.runtime.local.supervisor")`, and the `kora-runner` console script maps to the same `main`. The runner's argv is that command followed by exactly four arguments: the state-root path, the attempt id, the execution-domain id and the launch nonce; the workload never appears on it. `submit` starts a launcher, `python -I -c` with a fixed program, through `os.posix_spawn` with the signal mask cleared, `SIGPIPE`, `SIGXFSZ`, `SIGINT`, `SIGTERM`, `SIGCHLD`, `SIGHUP` and `SIGQUIT` at their defaults, descriptors 0, 1 and 2 opened on `/dev/null`, the readiness pipe's write end on descriptor 3, and a constructed environment of `PATH=/usr/bin:/bin`, `HOME`, `LANG` (`C.UTF-8` on Linux, `en_US.UTF-8` on macOS) and, on macOS, `TMPDIR`. The worker closes its copy of the write end at once. The launcher calls `setsid` itself, because CPython's `posix_spawn` has no `setsid` on Linux builds whose C library lacked `POSIX_SPAWN_SETSID` (the `uv`-managed interpreters among them); until it does, it is briefly a member of the worker's dispatch group and session, where a group signal only ends it (the worker then sees end of file) and a group scan only sees one more live member; it then closes every descriptor above 3, forks once and exits, so the runner is reparented, is not a session or group leader and records `leads_group: false`. The worker collects only the launcher, by its own pid, through `kora.executor.launcher.ExitWatch`; it never waits on `-1`. Once the watch reports the exit it reaps with `waitpid(pid, 0)`, because on macOS the `kqueue` exit event can arrive before `WNOHANG` can collect the child, and the exited child is collectable at once; before that it uses only `WNOHANG`. A watch that cannot be created (`EMFILE`) leaves the launcher owned without one: every later settling tries to create it again, and until then the launcher is killed by pid, which cannot be reused while unreaped, and polled with `WNOHANG`. A `ChildProcessError` from the reap means the pid was already collected elsewhere and counts as reaped. `submit` reads one byte from the pipe, non-blocking, against 30 s on the injected `Clock`. The byte means the runner registered; end of file means it exited before registering, which `submit` sees at once; the deadline or the caller's cancellation leaves the entry in `starting`. On each of those the launcher is settled: after a byte or end of file the worker waits up to 5 s for its exit; after a timeout or a cancellation it first kills it through the watch; the settling is shielded from the caller's cancellation, and a launcher still alive after the 5 s bound stays owned and is killed and collected by the next `submit` or by `close`. `close` first stops new spawns, so a `submit` whose transition was already running when `close` began is refused with `runtime_closed` before it spawns, then waits for every spawn still waiting for readiness, at most the 30 s deadline and its 5 s settling, and for every settling, then retries the owned launchers once with the same bound; one still alive after that is left to the worker's exit, and the registry and state-root descriptors are closed whether or not this settling fails. `submit` then returns the handle after the byte and raises the retryable `runner_starting` otherwise. The provider logs `runtime.local.spawn` with the outcome (`ready`, `ended`, `timeout`, `cancelled`, `failed` or `closed`) and the wait in milliseconds: INFO for `ready`, WARNING otherwise.
- **The runner entry** (issue #313). Before any thread starts the runner sets `umask(0o077)`, `chdir("/")` and `RLIMIT_CORE` 0, and on Linux `PR_SET_CHILD_SUBREAPER` and `PR_SET_DUMPABLE` 0. It opens the state root, creates `attempts/<name>/` at mode 0700 if missing, points `faulthandler` at `attempts/<name>/runner.crash` and configures the shared diagnostics bootstrap onto `attempts/<name>/runner.log` under the component `runtime.local.runner`, bound to the attempt id only; a failure before then leaves only its exit status. It reads its own identity through `kora.runner.identity`, checks `domain.json` names the execution domain of its argv, and reads the entry: anything but `starting` with its own nonce and no fence makes it exit without binding. It re-opens the recorded socket directory with the same checks, `fchdir`s to that descriptor, binds its socket by name, returns to `/` and listens without serving; the diagnostics writer thread already runs then, and it writes only through its descriptor, so the brief change of directory affects no other lookup. It then compare-and-sets `starting` to `running` with its own nonce and no fence, recording its `ProcessIdentity` and the socket path and keeping the submitter record. A lost compare-and-set, a fence or a failed registration write (`ENOSPC` included) unlinks the socket and exits without writing. Only a successful registration writes the readiness byte; `EPIPE`, because the worker gave up, is not fatal. Until #315 the runner holds no workload and serves nothing; until #197 it runs until it is killed by verified identity.
- **`submit`'s checks** (issue #313). Before any persistent effect `submit` checks, in this order: the attempt id (`attempt_invalid`); `Workload.execution_domain_id` against the runtime's (`execution_domain_mismatch`); `Workload.dispatcher_process` against the submitting process's own identity, read once when the runtime opens and compared by the full identity key, a null one included (`dispatcher_process_mismatch`); the request digest, `"sha256:"` followed by the lower-case hexadecimal SHA-256 of `canonical_bytes(Workload.execution_request)`, computed by `kora.executor.digests.object_digest`, the one digest function of canonical JSON, against `Workload.execution_request_digest` and against `ProtocolContext.request_digest` when that is set; and `Workload.deadline` against `ProtocolContext.deadline`. A digest or deadline difference is `workload_conflict`; the other refusals are not retryable. It then re-checks the socket directory, which recreates a directory a temporary-file cleaner removed. The API's sealing (work items 9 and 10) must produce the same digest. The field-by-field check of the other logical workload fields lands with the sealed-request schema in work item 10.
- **Registry.** Per-attempt files are named by `<name>`, the first 32 lower-case hexadecimal characters (128 bits) of the SHA-256 of the canonical JSON array `[execution_domain_id, attempt_id]`, because an attempt id may contain `/`, `.` and 256 characters; the head stores the full attempt and domain ids and is refused with `lineage_unavailable` when they differ (issue #312). An attempt id that is not 1 to 256 printable ASCII characters is refused with the non-retryable `attempt_invalid` before any file is touched (issue #323). `registry/<name>.json` is replaced atomically (write `registry/<name>.json.tmp-<nonce>` with a 128-bit nonce, created `O_EXCL|O_NOFOLLOW` mode 0600, `fsync`, or `F_FULLFSYNC` on macOS, rename, then the same sync of the directory); a temporary file whose write or fsync failed is unlinked before the lock is released, each write first removes the attempt's leftover temporary files, and a temporary file left by a crash is removed at the next lock acquisition for that attempt and at `release`. Each attempt has its own lock file, `registry/<name>.lock`, opened `O_NOFOLLOW|O_NONBLOCK` and checked as a private regular file like every state file. Each try takes `LOCK_NB` inside one job of a bounded transition executor (4 workers, at most 64 admitted jobs, beyond which a transition is refused with retryable `registry_busy`) and, when it succeeds, runs the read-compare-write and releases the lock; a busy lock returns at once, and the caller waits 10 ms on the injected clock between tries until the 2 s deadline, then raises retryable `registry_busy` and logs `runtime.local.lock_timeout`. No thread waits on a lock, queue time is never hold time, and a transition once started runs to completion although its caller was cancelled. A registry write that fails refuses its operation with retryable `registry_unavailable`; a head that cannot be read or parsed, or exceeds 64 KiB, is `lineage_unavailable`. State files are read up to their bound plus one byte and refused past it. A lock is held only for the read-compare-write of one transition, never across a spawn, the readiness wait, a retirement grace or a runner call; reads take no lock, because the atomic rename keeps them consistent; the stop path and kill escalation never wait on a lock (ADR-0022). The lock file is kept with the tombstone. The JSON file is the compact registry head. Immutable GrantBinding/DispatcherClosure records, prefix indexes and delivery dispositions live in separate durable indexed per-attempt records under the [resumable-history contract](#resumable-retained-dispatcher-history-issue-226). Bounded page/checkpoint transactions are crash-consistent; the head never loads or rewrites all retained history. Records survive retirement/release, and evidence_ref/record_ref resolve those retained records. The quiescence and fence metadata remain in the compact head. The runner checks for the fence under the lock immediately before starting any candidate.

| state | holds | written by |
|---|---|---|
| `unsubmitted` | the deadline binding (null included), the closed gate, dispatcher records | `workspace_target`, or the first evidence or acknowledgement recorded before any `submit` |
| `starting` | launch nonce, request digest, the submitter record `{dispatcher_id, process}`, started at, the socket directory, the runner prefix (the launch command followed by the state-root path, at most 64 non-empty arguments and 16 KiB, ending in an absolute path), the deadline binding and closed gate, an optional fence that `retire` writes before its sweep | `submit`, before spawn, from no entry or by compare-and-set from `unsubmitted` |
| `running` | runner `ProcessIdentity` (kept in every later state), socket path, the submitter record carried from `starting`, dispatcher records | the runner, by compare-and-set from `starting` with its own nonce and no fence; otherwise it exits without starting a candidate |
| `completed` | the normal-completion quiescence record; a fence | `quiescence` |
| `retiring` | the fence | `retire` |
| `retired` | the tombstone: receipt, scope, dispatcher evidence, the normal-completion record if any, capped samples with counts; compact head at most 64 KiB plus separate retained encoded binding/closure/index records at most 16 KiB per grant; no whole-history rewrite | `retire` (from no entry, `unsubmitted` or `starting` with scope `no_runner_started`), the runner finishing `retire` from `running`, `completed` or `retiring`, the runner after a full-disk stop, or the worker-side fallback |

`released_at` marks `release` on a `completed` or `retired` entry.

| operation | no entry, `unsubmitted` | `starting` | `running` | `completed` | `retiring` | `retired` | after `release` |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `submit` (same digest) | moves to `starting` | retryable until the runner registers | returns the handle | returns the handle | `attempt_retired` | `attempt_retired` | `attempt_retired` |
| `transfer_in`, `start`, `finalize` | refused: nothing submitted | retryable | allowed by phase; `journal_unavailable` under the full-disk fence | refused by the fence | refused by the fence | refused by the fence | `attempt_retired` |
| `workspace_target` | no entry writes `unsubmitted` with the binding; an equal binding returns the target, a different one is `workload_conflict` | equal binding: the target; different: `workload_conflict` | as `starting` | as `starting` | `attempt_retired` | `attempt_retired` | `attempt_retired` |
| `lookup` | `None` | retryable | the handle | the handle | the handle | the handle, also for a tombstone written before any `submit` | the handle, never `None` |
| `status` | no handle (`attempt_not_submitted`) | retryable | the runner's observation | `exited`, phase `complete`, never `lost` | the last observation | `exited`, the tombstone's phase, `failed` for `no_runner_started` | as recorded |
| `quiescence` | refused: nothing submitted | retryable | allowed once the candidate exited; `journal_unavailable` under the full-disk fence | the recorded record, worker-side once the runner has exited | refused by the fence | the recorded record, if the entry completed first | as recorded |
| `retire` | writes the fence and a `no_runner_started` tombstone | writes the fence, sweeps for the launch nonce, then tombstones `no_runner_started` | allowed; retryable under the full-disk fence until the tombstone is written | allowed; the tombstone keeps the normal-completion record | continues the retirement; retryable until it ends | the recorded receipt | the recorded receipt of a retired entry; `attempt_retired` for a released completed entry, whose normal-completion record stands |
| `recovery` | the recorded dispatcher evidence | the recorded evidence, unresolved | allowed; unresolved under the full-disk fence | evidence with `normal_completion` | evidence, the retirement unresolved | evidence with the receipt | as recorded |
| journal, log and output reads | nothing to read | nothing to read | allowed | allowed | allowed | allowed | `attempt_retired` |
| `record_dispatcher_quiescence` | worker-side, under the lock | worker-side, under the lock | through the runner, or worker-side once the runner is gone; `journal_unavailable` under the full-disk fence | through the runner, or worker-side once the runner has exited | worker-side, into the entry | worker-side, into the tombstone | `attempt_retired` |

- **The deadline binding** (ADR-0022). `workspace_target` takes the binding from `ProtocolContext.deadline` and writes it, null included, into an `unsubmitted` entry under the lock. Expiry is observed when the context clock, read inside the locked transition, reaches `deadline.at - deadline.clock_uncertainty`, so a lock retry cannot grant a binding past it; it writes the sticky closed gate under the lock and refuses with `deadline_expired`, and every later `workspace_target` or `submit` under that gate is refused the same way. `submit` refuses a `Workload.deadline` that differs from the binding with `workload_conflict`, and from no entry binds `Workload.deadline`. The transition cells are `kora.runner.registry`'s (issue #312) and `kora.plugins.runtime.local.runtime.LocalRuntime` applies them (issue #323). `workspace_target` returns `root` and `host_path` equal to `<state_root>/workspaces/<name>`: advisory text that this runtime does not create. The part of #195 that allocates the workspace creates and opens it relative to the state-root descriptor; which part that is remains open.
- **Interim cells (issue #195).** Until the runner's retirement lands (#197), `retire` on a `running`, `completed` or `retiring` entry raises the retryable `retirement_unavailable` and writes nothing; until the control channel lands (#315), `status` on a `running` or `retiring` entry is the retryable `runner_unreachable`, which this section already allows for an unreachable runner, and a submitted runner holds no workload; until the service-manager checks land (#316), the Linux socket directory is always `/tmp/kora-<uid>`; `runner_starting` is the retryable refusal in `starting`.
- **Runtime lifecycle** (issue #323). A handle is this runtime's only when it equals the handle the runtime builds for its attempt id: `runtime` `local`, this `execution_domain_id`, `data` `{"format": "local/1"}` and a null `host_hint`; any other handle is the non-retryable `handle_invalid`. The runtime's owner, the worker host, closes it. `close` stops admitting work at once, drains the transitions already running in the executor (at most 4 running and 64 admitted, each one lock try and one synced write) and the sweeps in flight (at most four, issue #430), which stop at their next step; a same-user process that blocks the kernel's argv read can delay this drain, then closes the registry and state-root descriptors, which stay open if the drain itself is cancelled so that no running job loses its descriptor; it is bounded by filesystem latency rather than a deadline. Every call that reaches the registry after `close` began, by starting or by retrying, is refused with the non-retryable `runtime_closed`; `capabilities` still answers, and an attempt id or handle that fails its check is refused with its own code first; the worker never turns it into an attempt outcome, and the attempt is adopted by the next owner. A cancelled or concurrent `close` never skips the release. The context clock's `now()` is called from the executor's threads, so a clock passed to this runtime is safe to read from them.
- **Entries before submit.** With no entry, or in `unsubmitted`, `lookup` returns `None`, the authoritative absence of a handle, which certifies nothing about earlier dispatchers ([scheduler section 3](scheduler.md#3-submission-execution-uncertainty-and-recovery), step 5), and `recovery` returns the recorded dispatcher evidence. In `starting`, `lookup` is retryable: until the spawn deadline (30 s) and after it, until `retire`, which writes the fence, sweeps for the launch nonce, checking identity before signalling, kills any match, and only then tombstones with scope `no_runner_started`, a choice made from registry state, never from whether the API has a handle. The sweep is [issue #430](https://github.com/realkarych/kora/issues/430)'s. Its vector is the recorded runner prefix followed by the entry's attempt id, execution-domain id and nonce, the vector `submit` spawned. It lists the processes, other than the worker, whose real, effective and saved uid are the worker's and whose complete argv equals the vector. On Linux both are read relative to one `/proc/<pid>` descriptor, the uids from the `Uid:` line of `status` before `cmdline` is read, so a pid reused between the two reads fails instead of exposing another process's argv. On macOS `proc_listpids(PROC_RUID_ONLY)` and `PROC_PIDT_SHORTBSDINFO` select the processes, so no other uid's argv is read, and `sysctl(KERN_PROCARGS2)` is parsed exactly: the argument count must equal the vector's, the arguments start at the first 8-byte boundary after the executable path, and only that many bytes are copied, so no environment string is materialised. Argv bytes are compared in memory and never logged, raised or retained. The listing and the pinning of its matches are bounded by 1 s and by the scan capacity, and more than 8 matches refuse. Each match is pinned by an exit watch (a pidfd on Linux, a `kqueue` exit event on macOS) before its identity, its uids and its argv are read again and its identity once more; only a match whose two reads agree and whose watch has not yet fired is signalled, with `SIGKILL`, through the pidfd on Linux, so a reused pid is never signalled; on macOS a pid reused between the last read and `kill` remains a residual window, and a watch that already saw the exit sends nothing. A watch that cannot be opened is followed by an identity read, and only an absent or dead process is dropped. The grace bounds the kill and its confirmation: when it has already passed, a verified match is refused without a signal, so a zero or negative grace with a live match always refuses; otherwise each killed match is confirmed by its exit watch, or, for a watch that did not fire before the grace ended, by an identity read showing it absent, changed or dead. An incomplete listing, an unreadable match, a failed signal or a match not confirmed within the grace raises the retryable `runner_stop_unconfirmed` with empty details, writes no receipt and keeps the fence, so the next `retire` repeats the sweep under the same retirement id. At most four sweeps run at once, and a fifth is refused with the retryable `registry_busy` before it starts; their reads run in a second executor of one worker, so they never occupy the lock-transition workers. Once `close` begins, a sweep signals nothing more: one waiting for exits stops waiting, and every sweep ends with `runtime_closed`, leaving the fence. The `no_runner_started` claim rests on the fence written first: a runner the listing misses, before its launcher `exec`s it or because it rewrote its argv, cannot register and starts no candidate. The provider logs `runtime.local.retire` for each sweep that ends in a tombstone (INFO) or a refusal (WARNING), with `outcome` (`tombstoned`, `listing_incomplete`, `match_unreadable`, `signal_unknown`, `too_many_matches` or `survived_grace`), `matched` (the processes listed as carrying the vector), `killed` (the signals delivered) and `waited_ms`, and no attempt id, path, pid or argv; a sweep refused before it starts, a cancelled sweep, one ended by `runtime_closed` and one whose tombstone write is refused log nothing. `retire` with no entry writes the tombstone itself, so a delayed `submit` finds the fence and returns `attempt_retired`. `submit` never respawns under an existing entry; a different request digest is `workload_conflict`. `lookup` returns the immutable `Handle` rebuilt from the entry.
- **Exit during pinning (issue #508).** If the exit watch completes while the matching identity read returns, pinning makes one fresh identity read through the same sweep executor. Only an absent or dead process is dropped; an unreadable observation or any live identity, including a changed identity, refuses. This branch never signals, and the completed watch alone cannot authorize ignoring a live successor. The [regression record](../research/runtime-retirement-ready-watch-2026-10-30.md) distinguishes the proven race from historically unattributed hosted failures.
- **Worker-side acknowledgements.** With no runner, the worker-side runtime appends an acknowledgement under the registry lock only when the caller's own `ProcessIdentity` equals the process the runner recorded for that grant or, for a grant that never reached the runner, the bound process of the `GrantBinding` passed with it; this check is cooperative on local.

**The control channel.**

- **Socket directory.** On macOS `confstr(_CS_DARWIN_USER_TEMP_DIR)/kora`; on Linux `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR/kora` when the user manager lingers, otherwise `/tmp/kora-<uid>`. The path fits the 104-byte `sun_path` of macOS, and abstract sockets are never used. The directory is opened `O_DIRECTORY|O_NOFOLLOW` and checked owner 0700 with `fstat`; anything else is refused. On macOS the value is read with `os.confstr(65537)`; its leading `/var` is rewritten to `/private/var` only after `lstat` shows `/var` is a root-owned symlink to `private/var`, and every other component is walked without following links, with the state root's ancestor checks (issue #313). Only the last component is created if missing, by `mkdir` at mode 0700 relative to its no-follow-opened parent; a missing parent is refused. The check runs when the runtime opens and again in every `submit`; every failure, an over-long path included, is the non-retryable `socket_directory_unsafe`, with a fixed message naming the remedy, empty details and no chained error. The directory is a keyword argument of `LocalRuntime.open`, and an injected directory goes through the same checks. The runner re-verifies its socket's inode every 60 s and rebinds it if the path was removed, repeating the checks if it recreates the directory. A refused or missing socket while the registry says `running` is retryable, never `lost`. The socket name is a 16-character hash of the execution domain id and the attempt id.
- **Frames.** A 4-byte big-endian payload length and a 1-byte type (`J` JSON, `B` bytes, `E` journal record), at most 1 MiB per frame. The receiver reads the length and the type through `kora.runner.channels`' reservations before it allocates the payload: a frame whose type the reader does not expect, whose length exceeds its reservation (8 KiB for the frame before attach, the operation's header cap for a sectioned header, 64 KiB for a section frame) or whose payload ends early closes the connection (issue #314).
- **Sectioned messages** (issue #314). A message larger than one frame, such as a `Workload` at its field maxima (about 11.9 MB), crosses the channel as one header `J` frame `{op, request_id, sections: [{name, length, sha256}]}` followed by each section's bytes in `B` frames of 1 to 64 KiB, in the declared order; an empty section has no `B` frame. No `B` frame spans two sections. The operation fixes the header cap, the exact section names in order, each once, and each section's byte and JSON-depth caps; `accept_workload` (#315) declares one section, `workload`, of at most 12 MiB and depth 18, under an 8 KiB header, and `adopt` and `recovery` (#196, #197) declare theirs from `kora.protocols.history.CONTENT_SECTIONS`. The receiver refuses before any allocation a header over its cap, a header that is not strict JSON, a wrong operation, and an extra, missing, reordered or misnamed section or a length over its cap. It then receives each section into one buffer of exactly the declared length, hashing as it receives. It refuses a frame that spans into the next section, an empty frame, a section that ends early and a SHA-256 mismatch. Progress is bounded on the injected `Clock`: the header within 5 s, each `B` frame within 5 s of the previous frame and the whole message within 60 s of the header's start. A section's JSON is decoded only after every section has been received and verified. The strict decode refuses duplicate members, non-finite constants, invalid scalars and nesting past the section's depth. The same bytes are then validated with the model's JSON-mode validation, because the strict protocol models accept ISO-8601 durations and instants only from JSON text. No partial typed value is returned, and a refusal keeps no validation error or parsed input alive. **Accepted limitation, revisited in #315:** the section caps bound the bytes received, not the memory and CPU spent decoding them. A well-formed, shallow section at its cap made of dense empty containers drives the strict decode and the model validation to hundreds of MiB, and an unknown member holding such content to several GiB, before the section is refused. A valid maximal `Workload` costs about 0.1 GB and 0.2 s. A cap on the number of containers derived from the model would still admit about 1.75 million containers in a valid `Workload`'s five 1 MiB JSON fields, so it would not change the order of the cost. Until #315 nothing serves the codec. #315 brings one workload-sized message per runner, decoding on the runner's one-thread executor and the attested same-user peer, and decides then whether a schema-aware pre-check is needed. The codec-peak diagnostic records a dense-container section beside the maximal `Workload`. Every refusal raises `message_refused` with the detail `reason` (`capacity`, `malformed`, `integrity`, `deadline` or `closed`), retryable only for `deadline` and `closed`, and no envelope answers a refused message. A refusal while receiving closes the connection inside the codec, because the remaining frames can no longer be delimited. Decoding runs after the receive and holds no connection, so the serving loop closes the connection that carried a section it refused to decode (#315). The section digests are integrity checks only; authority comes from the attested peer.
- **Response envelope** (issue #314). Every response is one `J` frame. A `KoraProtocolError` of any operation is answered `{request_id, error: {code, message, retryable, details}}`. `recovery` answers `{request_id, result, value}`, with `result` set to `"progress"` or `"page"` (#197), so #262's models carry no discriminator. Each other operation's result form is fixed by the issue that implements the operation (#315, #196, #197). Each code that may appear in an envelope is allowlisted with its reviewed message text, its retryability and its typed details. The writer sends the allowlisted text, never the raised error's text. It refuses as a programming error a code, a retryability or a detail outside the allowlist, so no exception text, validation error or input value reaches the peer. This part allowlists only `operation_unknown`: not retryable, without details, so an unknown operation's name is not echoed. `message_refused` is never sent. The reader applies the same allowlist and refuses any other code, message, retryability or detail as `message_refused` with reason `malformed`. The operations of #315, #196 and #197 add their codes to the allowlist.
- **Dispatcher peers.** The worker's incarnation is authenticated by the socket directory's 0700 mode, the kernel's peer attestation below, a peer uid equal to the runner's, and its attested `ProcessIdentity`; there is no shared secret, because same-user code could read one. Its first frame names `dispatcher_id` and `incarnation_id`. The worker authenticates the runner the same way before it sends that frame: the listening peer's attested `ProcessIdentity` must equal the registry's `running` runner identity, and its uid the worker's own. `submit` records the submitter `{dispatcher_id, process}` in `starting`, where `process` equals `Workload.dispatcher_process`; neither `Workload` nor `ProtocolContext` carries an incarnation, so the runner records it at attach (issue #313, amending #195's workload-transfer decision on 2026-10-29), and #315 compares the attested peer with the full identity and the attach's `dispatcher_id` with the recorded one. On first attach the runner records `(dispatcher_id, incarnation_id, attested ProcessIdentity)`, which never changes; a later attach under that id from another process is refused, and the attested identity must equal the grant's bound process from `Workload` or `recovery`, a binding recorded at `submit` or presented separately as the API claim's current_grant before history transfer; the runner cannot tell a presented binding from one a same-user process supplies naming itself, which the threat model's `local` row states. A `registry_acknowledgement` is accepted only from the recorded process.
- **Launcher peers** hold a one-shot per-launch capability and may call only `launch_spec` for that launch (section 3).
- **Connection bounds.** A connection that has not completed its handshake (a dispatcher's attach, or a launcher peer's capability and the checks of step 2 of the harness launcher in section 3) within 5 s is closed; a launcher connection that has completed it stays open until `exec`, a failure record or the session's escalation; at most 16 connections per attempt are open at once, of which at most 4 follow `events`. `events` streams from the journal file one frame at a time, with no buffer per follower beyond that frame.
- **Kernel attestation and signalling.** The peer is attested by the kernel (`LOCAL_PEERPID` with `LOCAL_PEERCRED` on macOS, where when the pid is captured is verified by the runner work item, `SO_PEERCRED` on Linux, `SO_PEERPIDFD` where available). On Linux with `SO_PEERPIDFD`, the identity is read through that pidfd (its pid from the pidfd's `fdinfo`), and a pidfd whose process has exited, checked again after the start time is read, is refused, so a reused pid cannot carry the attestation. Without a pidfd (macOS, older Linux), the peer is refused when the start time looked up for its pid is later than the connection's accept time, both on one boot-relative clock (`/proc/<pid>/stat` ticks against `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` on Linux; `proc_pid_rusage` `ri_proc_start_abstime` against `mach_absolute_time` on macOS) with the start time rounded up to the next tick, so rounding refuses rather than accepts, and the lookup is repeated after the comparison. That still leaves a window: a peer can connect, hand its descriptor on and exit, and its pid can be reused before the accept; this residual is stated in the threat model's `local` row. On the worker's side the kernel reports the listener's identity as of `listen`, not as of the connection, so the worker anchors no time window there: it requires the attested pid's current identity (through its pidfd where available, otherwise its start time read after the connection) to equal the registry's `running` runner identity, pid and start time, so a reused pid fails the comparison, and it closes the connection without sending its first frame otherwise. A peer pid of 0, which a peer outside the runner's pid namespace reports, is refused. Signals to a single process go through `pidfd_send_signal` where available, or after a start-time recheck immediately before signalling. The runner signals a leader's group only once the leader is verified to lead its own group (pgid equal to pid), and then only while it holds the leader unreaped or after a recheck that the leader is alive; on Linux it uses `PIDFD_SIGNAL_PROCESS_GROUP` on the leader's pidfd, under the same precondition, where the kernel has it. On macOS, and on Linux without that flag, a race between the recheck and a group signal sent without the unreaped hold remains.
- **Operations.** `status`, `heartbeat`, `adopt`, `grant_history_status`, `accept_grant_history`, `recovery`, `transfer_in`, `start`, `events{from}`, `transfer_out`, `cancel`, `finalize` (carrying `PublicationAuthorization`), `quiescence`, `retire`, `record_dispatcher_quiescence`. The kill handle is runner-internal and on no channel. Each incarnation keeps one persistent control connection; `control.attached` is journalled only on `adopt`, refused authentications are coalesced into one counted record, and the runner watches every connection, including one blocked on `events`, for end of stream.

**The journal.**

- **Format.** One canonical JSON line per `{i, at, kind, data}` record, on `O_APPEND`; `i` is assigned at append, and a reader ignores a trailing partial line. The supervisor kinds are `phase`, `runner.started`, `runner.candidate_started`, `runner.outputs_sealed`, `runner.quiescence`, `runner.retired`, `runner.journal_gap`, `runner.cap_reached`, `runtime.retired_without_runner`, `transfer.materialised`, `transfer.rejected`, `control.attached`, `control.detached`, `control.auth_failed` and `dispatcher.quiescence`; `EventSink` rejects all of them from adapters (section 3).
- **Writing.** One writer thread performs every write. `checkpoint.json` is replaced atomically like the registry entry, its temporary files handled the same way. Supervisor records, registry writes and `checkpoint.json` are durable before acknowledgement (`fdatasync` on Linux, `F_FULLFSYNC` on macOS), a few records per attempt, so tombstones and fences survive power loss; a sync over 5 s ends the attempt as `journal_unavailable`. Runner exit and stop escalation never join or wait on the writer thread, and diagnostics that cannot be written are dropped with a count instead of blocking. Adapter records reach the writer through the bounded queue of section 3 ("`EventSink` and journal back-pressure") and are batched every 50 ms or 256 records, the timer armed only while the batch is non-empty and the batch flushed before each supervisor record, so file order is `i` order; they survive a process crash once written, records still queued are lost with the runner, and the power-loss limit applies to them only.
- **Caps.** The journal is capped at 256 MiB, the stdout and stderr logs at 64 MiB each and `runner.log` (one writer) at 16 MiB; interpreter crash output goes to `runner.crash`, written only by `faulthandler` and fatal output, at most 1 MiB. Past a cap the runner writes one `runner.cap_reached` record and keeps draining the candidate's pipes, counting the discarded bytes. At the journal cap adapter records go to `runner.journal_gap`; supervisor records are still appended up to 1 MiB past the cap, beyond which only the kinds written at most once per attempt (`phase` once per phase, `runner.outputs_sealed`, `runner.quiescence`, `runner.retired` and `runtime.retired_without_runner`) are still appended, and every other kind is counted in memory and written once, as one counted record per kind, with the terminal supervisor records. At most 1,000 transfer items are allowed per attempt, which bounds the synced records. These values are proposals.
- **A full disk.** A runner whose registration write (`starting` to `running`) fails exits without starting a candidate and writes nothing, leaving the entry to `retire`'s `starting` path. In every state, the first journal append or sync that fails with `ENOSPC` or `EDQUOT` closes the journal for the process that saw the failure: that process appends nothing more to it, readers treat it as ending at its last complete line (a complete line whose sync failed stays readable and is counted in the tombstone as not durable), as after a crash, no `i` is reused, every `events` stream ends with a `journal_unavailable` frame, and that process's later records go only into the tombstone, as counts and the evidence they carry. A later writer in another process, the worker-side fallback, repairs the tail as a second writer below and continues `i` from the last complete line. Diagnostic and log writes are dropped with a count instead, and a failed index write disables the index, so readers scan. Outside `running`, and for a worker-side registry write in any state, a registry or journal write that fails that way refuses only its operation with a retryable error; the outcome and any normal-completion record stand, and `retire` stays retryable until its tombstone is written. In `running`, when a write of a supervisor record, an adapter-record batch, the registry or `checkpoint.json` fails that way, the runner, in this order: holds an in-memory fence, which needs no disk, that refuses `start`, `transfer_in`, `finalize`, `quiescence` and `record_dispatcher_quiescence` with a retryable `journal_unavailable` error, even while the entry still reads `running`, so nothing starts during the stop and a full-disk-stopped attempt is never recorded as a normal completion, while an authenticated reconnect to an already durable binding remains observational; a first attach/adoption requiring a new undurable peer/grant record refuses retryably with `journal_unavailable`; stops the candidate domain and any publisher domain (the stop path needs no disk), so a `finalize` in progress ends with its outcome `unknown` and recovery reports the publication for reconciliation ([ADR-0017](../../docs/decisions/0017-execution-uncertainty-and-safe-replacement.md)); and closes the journal. The terminal evidence then goes only into the tombstone: the stop evidence, references to already durable dispatcher records, and the counts of the records not journalled (queued and later adapter records, and the supervisor records that would have followed), held in memory and bounded like the tombstone. The attempt fails with `journal_unavailable` (`infrastructure`). Until the tombstone is written, the runner retries it after 1 s and then doubling up to 30 s. Under the fence, `retire` starts no write of its own: it joins the runner's next scheduled retry, which writes its fence with the tombstone, and returns a retryable error until that retry succeeds. The runtime deletes nothing to make space: the workspace, the placements and the transcript stay in place for `release`. When the runner is gone, the worker-side fallback runs its own sweep, and its receipt carries only that sweep's scope; its journal closes on a failed append or sync in the same way, and its record then goes into the tombstone only. If the tombstone cannot be written, the worker-side runtime keeps the sweep result in memory in tombstone form (capped samples with counts, within the tombstone bound) and owns one retry timer per attempt with the same backoff; each retry reads and updates only the compact head under the lock, preserving references to the separate durable dispatcher records and their current index, so it neither copies history nor loses worker-side acknowledgements or process-exit evidence recorded meanwhile; `retire` joins that timer and returns a retryable error until it succeeds, worker shutdown cancels the timer without joining it, drops the held result and writes nothing, and a restarted worker runs one new sweep. Until the tombstone is written, the runner keeps its socket and connections, `kora doctor` reports the attempt as stopped on a full disk for an operator to free space, and each failed write is a WARNING diagnostic, coalesced with a count. Recovery stays unresolved and retryable meanwhile; no evidence is claimed that was not written.
- **The stop path** never waits on the writer or a lock; with the journal unavailable, the stop evidence lands in the registry tombstone once it can be written. `control.detached` and the coalesced `control.auth_failed` record are written at most once per incarnation.
- **A second writer.** Before the worker-side fallback appends, under the lock with the runner confirmed dead, it repairs the journal: it truncates it to its last newline and records the number of discarded bytes. It appends `runtime.retired_without_runner` only when the journal holds none, so a restarted fallback never writes it twice.
- **Index and reads.** A sparse offset index every 4,096 records lets `events{from}` resume near a cap without a full scan. Once the runner is gone, the worker-side runtime reads the journal tail, the logs and the transcript descriptor-relative under the same caps and digests and relays them as segments.

**Transfer.** `TransferItem` names one item. Kind `script` carries a fake harness script to the testing runner of [fakes.md section 3](fakes.md#3-the-testing-runner-and-the-scenario-composition): it is transferred once, in `preparation`, and materialised under the runner's control directory outside every candidate-visible root, and the production runner refuses it. Its `name` is a single path component with no `/`, `..`, NUL or control characters and is never empty. Proposed limits are 1 GiB per item and 4 GiB per attempt; issue #106 owns input semantics.

- **`transfer_in`** is allowed only in `preparation`, and the worker streams from a spool file, never from memory. The runner writes `O_CREAT|O_EXCL|O_NOFOLLOW` beneath the input-root descriptor, hashing while it writes. On a match it fsyncs, sets mode 0400, renames with no replace (`RENAME_NOREPLACE` or `RENAME_EXCL`), fsyncs the directory and journals `transfer.materialised`. On a mismatch, a disconnect or a cancel it removes the temporary file; a mismatch is journalled `transfer.rejected` and fails with `integrity_error`. A different digest under an existing name is `workload_conflict`.
- **Sealing** resolves each output path beneath the output-root descriptor with `openat2(RESOLVE_BENEATH|RESOLVE_NO_SYMLINKS)` on Linux and a component-by-component `openat(O_NOFOLLOW|O_DIRECTORY)` walk on macOS, so no intermediate symlink or `..` is followed; it opens the file `O_NOFOLLOW|O_NONBLOCK`, accepts only regular files (`S_ISREG`) on the root's device (`st_dev`) with one link (`st_nlink` 1) within per-file and total bounds, and records symlinks, hard-linked files, FIFOs and other special files as refused. It journals `runner.outputs_sealed`, a SHA-256 manifest of at most 1,000 entries, which keeps the record below the frame cap.
- **`transfer_out`** accepts only manifest names and streams 64 KiB frames, then a trailer digest. The worker spools to a file and keeps it only if the received digest, the trailer and the manifest agree, and deletes its input and output spools on success, failure and cancel. Each incarnation has its own spool directory, and worker start-up sweeps only directories whose incarnation's `ProcessIdentity` is gone; `kora doctor` checks free space under the state root. A pull is retried from the sealed source at most 3 times, then fails with `infrastructure`. Of the control directory only the two logs can be exported.

**Local provider evidence closes a dead worker's dispatcher.** `runtime_observed_process_exit` holds for a grant only when every condition holds:

1. **A bound identity.** The grant has a bound `ProcessIdentity`; a null process yields no evidence.
2. **The same host.** The `host_id` is present and equal to this host's; otherwise the grant stays unresolved.
3. **The process is gone.** On Linux and macOS alike, if the `boot_id` differs and appears in this state root's boot log, the process has exited and the remaining conditions are not needed; a `boot_id` the log does not know, as on a cloned host, leaves the grant unresolved. Otherwise the pid namespace must be equal (else unresolved), and the pid must be absent or its start time differ.
4. **Its dispatch group is empty.** No process remains in the process group whose id is the bound pid. At start-up the worker checks `getpgid(0) == getpid()`, otherwise calls `setpgid(0, 0)`, or refuses to start, and its registered identity records `leads_group: true`; an identity without that record supports no exit evidence. Every effect subprocess it starts (a `worker_host` executor, `git`) stays in that group, and kill escalation for such a subprocess signals its verified pid through a pidfd or after a start-time recheck, never a separate group. A group's id cannot be reused while a member exists, so a surviving child that stays in the group keeps the grant open. Effect subprocesses run without detaching: `git` with `gc.autoDetach=false` and `maintenance.autoDetach=false` and without the credential-cache daemon, `ssh` with `ControlMaster=no`, and no agent auto-start. A descendant that still leaves the group with `setsid` or `setpgid` is not observed, and its effects escape the evidence; this limit is stated with the evidence.
5. **The runner holds nothing from it.** The runner has no open connection from that process, and every request it received from it has completed, or been aborted and journalled or, with the journal closed, held for the tombstone. Right after a death the runner refuses retryably until this holds, and the worker retries against a 5 s deadline; a refusal is never closure.

The evidence covers grants that never reached the runner, because the bound identity comes from the API's grant list; without a runner, the local runtime evaluates conditions 1 to 4 under the registry lock and appends the evidence to the entry. It carries the observed identity, which the API compares with the grant. It does not prove that the dead worker's worker-side effects completed: those keep the idempotent-submit and registry-lock rules, and publication still needs reconciliation ([ADR-0017](../../docs/decisions/0017-execution-uncertainty-and-safe-replacement.md), clarified 2026-10-21).

**Honest receipts.** `TerminationReceipt.scope` and `NormalCompletionQuiescence.scope` name what was observed, and `attempt.recovery` records it. Quiescence holds within the recorded scope, and local scopes are cooperative.

| scope | where | stated limit |
|---|---|---|
| `subreaper+leader_group+env_sweep` | Linux; the runner is `PR_SET_CHILD_SUBREAPER` | non-dumpable processes hide their environment; a process that clears or overwrites its environment, or is started through an existing same-user daemon (the user manager over D-Bus, a terminal multiplexer server), is not seen |
| `leader_group+env_sweep` | macOS | a `setsid` descendant running a platform binary is invisible; a `setsid` descendant that clears or overwrites its environment, a group member that clears its environment once the leader has been reaped (no subreaper adopts it), or a process started through an existing same-user daemon, is not seen |
| `env_sweep` | the worker-side fallback when no leader identity is alive | as above, and any detached descendant without the token; same-user code that rewrites `attempt.token` hides the processes holding the real one and, after a worker restart, defeats the worker-side redaction of the real token |
| `no_runner_started` | registry state only | none: no candidate ever ran |

- **Leaders.** Each launch and each command is its own session and group leader, and the runner records every leader's identity.
- **Reaping.** One reaper, as subreaper on Linux, is woken by `SIGCHLD`, a pidfd per known child on Linux, or `kqueue` `EVFILT_PROC`/`NOTE_EXIT` on macOS, never by a `P_ALL` loop. On each wake-up it lists the runner's children (`/proc/self/task/*/children` on Linux; its own known children on macOS) and reaps only unregistered children (`waitid(P_PID, pid, WEXITED|WNOHANG)` on Linux; on macOS, where CPython has no `os.waitid`, `waitpid(pid, WNOHANG)` after the exit status was read from the `NOTE_EXITSTATUS` event), each after a 1 s grace from its first observed exit; a timer re-scans at the end of each grace and every 5 s while any child exists. It never touches a pid that an in-process waiter registered, including the SDK's own wait on the launch pid, which is registered when its launcher peer connects.
- **The worker-side fallback** applies when the runner is lost, and fails closed. It runs only once the runner's identity is confirmed gone; if the runner is alive but unresponsive, it first signals the runner by its verified identity. It signals only processes with original-group evidence: recorded leader identities still alive with pgid equal to pid, and processes holding `KORA_ATTEMPT_TOKEN` in a readable environment, with their identity rechecked before each signal. It never signals a bare numeric pgid. It issues a receipt only when its scope's condition holds (every leader is gone and the sweep is empty); when `attempt.token` is missing, unreadable or malformed, no environment sweep is performed, no receipt is issued and recovery stays unresolved; members of an original numeric pgid it cannot verify are listed by pid and start time only and leave recovery unresolved, with no receipt. It signals only individually verified processes. Environments and arguments read during the sweep are compared in memory and never retained in any record or diagnostic.
