The concrete implementation plan may choose another mechanism only if it proves the same identity, creator attribution and file/process effect closure across concurrent instances and restart. This witness selects no implementation and supplies no measured guarantee. For S3-compatible backends, conditional create and atomic creator metadata plus authoritative mutation closure are prerequisites; unsupported/uncertain backends fail closed. No generic S3 capability is assumed.

The storage work is linear in payload bytes and bounded part count, not constant I/O: streaming writes N bytes; sealing may read/hash N; core's mandatory immutable validation reread reads N; contiguous publication preparation may copy N once. Do not repeat full assembly on exact replay. The local witness can retain N bytes of parts plus N bytes of assembled content at peak; an additional hard link to the same inode does not allocate another payload copy but keeps that inode alive. Reserve provider capacity for the actual implementation's simultaneous incomplete/assembly copies before allocation; include read buffers, partial chunks and metadata overhead in measured memory/disk counters. Provider reads must not materialize a whole N-byte object in RAM. Accepted content is durable storage, while source parts/preparation links/operation receipts stay charged until their actual release. Core content-reader lifecycle remains separate: shared deletion can make future reads unavailable, while any existing open descriptor remains charged/owned until closed and cannot resurrect an expired metadata owner.

Deletion has the same acknowledgment/closure discipline as staging. Admission reserves one finite deletion slot before dispatch; repeated attempts with the same operation use that slot and bounded state, and saturation refuses before I/O. After core durably saves the outcome and closes future and earlier issuance, release_delete requires authoritative closure of every provider attempt. Its acknowledgment allows provider receipts and the matching core intent/generation to compact; handshake state stays charged until acknowledgment is durably known on both sides. No permanent per-generation history is required. A compact guard may remain only where its ownership and finite bound are stated; it cannot silently grow with lifetime traffic. After completed compaction an old operation is unavailable, never a fresh deletion against recreated content. Concrete issuer/effect closure is mandatory before removing its guard. Repeated delete/recreate cycles, lost responses, crash before/after acknowledgment and delayed old deletes must demonstrate stable settled receipt/intent counts and preserve the full-address fence. #216 owns the metadata side and local storage owns provider evidence; neither is supplied by the design PR.

After release, the core's saved outcome is the replay authority. A provider whose compacted operation evidence is unavailable returns an explicit unavailable observation, never a fabricated no-effect result or a new created_object value. release_stage is idempotent and cannot restart useful work. The implementation must demonstrate that its compacted closed-stage guard or complete issuer/effect closure prevents a replay from re-opening data; merely deleting all stage records is not a proof. This condition and physical identity durability are blocking criteria of the local implementation plan.

### 7.3. Implementations and activation

Built-in, both in MVP (FR-4.4, ADR-0005): `local` for standalone and development (directory tree `artifacts/sha256/ab/cd/<hex>`, `StorageRef = local://sha256/<hex>`, DD-36) and `s3` for docker-compose and organisation deployments, against any S3-compatible service through the `obstore` library (ADR-0013; objects under `<prefix>/sha256/ab/cd/<hex>` in one configured bucket, `StorageRef = s3://sha256/<hex>` resolved against the configured bucket and prefix so that references survive a bucket move; GCS and Azure are reachable through the same library without a new implementation). `s3` is the accepted organisation-deployment store; capacity and retention need sizing against measured traffic. The [load assumptions](../analysis/control-plane-load-estimate.md) do not establish that the volume cannot fit local disk. Workers never reach the store: they upload through the API, and the API always proxies downloads; presigned URLs were dropped from v1 (DD-37).

Local storage and its conformance/lifecycle evidence activate in the separately approved local-provider part of work item 8 after #229 and #224. Real API/metadata/retention evidence remains with work items 11/12/17 and [#216](https://github.com/realkarych/kora/issues/216); no provider-only result satisfies it. S3-specific conformance and actual closure guarantees activate with that backend. The implementation must prove conditional create, durable creator attribution and authoritative effect closure for its concrete service; an unsupported or uncertain capability fails closed. The accepted backend choice does not assert that every compatible service supplies these guarantees.

### 7.4. Local provider implementation contract (issue #255)

The [owner-approved local implementation plan](https://github.com/realkarych/kora/issues/255#issuecomment-6371758782) selects cooperative complete-issuer closure with a durable physical-root epoch. Each receiver captures its epoch immutably before issuing work. Exact settled receipts are resolved before checking the epoch; byte-bearing replay independently verifies the supplied bytes under finite verifier admission. Compacted receipts refuse before consuming input. New effects and readers require the current nonterminal epoch. Retirement closes admission before seeking exclusive actual effect/reader ownership. Release requires the exact core outcome revision, mutation-set digest and closed-issuance evidence, actual ownership closure and completed retirement; epoch advancement and receipt compaction commit together. Old receivers cannot recreate absent owners or cleanup guards. At the maximum count, admission closes permanently without wraparound; tracked cleanup remains possible. Absence never reconstructs a historical mutation outcome.

Core reconstruction is explicit composition work after checking durable issuance and lifecycle state. It closes the old receiver's actual jobs, streams and executors before replacement and allows at most three reconstructions within a finite request deadline. Useful dispatch requires open issuance. Unresolved cleanup may reconstruct the original retirement intent; after retirement is saved and release authorized, no new state-creating retirement is issued. Fully released owners permit observation, retained result replay and exact release acknowledgment only. These core obligations remain pending their real consumers, including #216; provider fixtures model them without claiming metadata durability.

The local provider permits one promotion operation and content generation per stage. A different generation requires a fresh stage with independently validated bytes, owner admission and private capacity. A uniquely owned prepared or assembled inode remains as the creator witness through durable outcome persistence. Recovery reconciles the persisted promotion intent against that witness before retirement can remove it. Unknown attribution remains charged. Content deletion refuses unresolved promotions; retained receipts preserve original creation attribution even after deletion.

The private root has a stable canonical control path, a durable random physical-store identity and a manifest binding device, inode, effective owner, format and immutable limits. Aliases and restarts share that identity. Copying the manifest, overlapping domains, changed anchors, uncertain restore/clone lineage or unreachable canonical control paths refuse; offline relocation and migration are not implemented. Initial supported filesystems are ext4 on Linux and APFS/HFS+ on macOS. Payload effects and cleanup use descriptor-relative no-follow operations. Provider-private SQLite uses its canonical path; live root/control replacement is unsupported cooperative operator behavior, and detected substitution refuses. This qualification does not promise race-safe refusal during SQLite internals.

Canonical ancestors must be owned by the effective user or root and not group/other writable, except a root-owned sticky directory protecting the next user/root-owned entry. Native ACL inspection accepts absent or deny-only ACLs and refuses allow or uninspectable ACLs. The root is 0700, private files 0600, and configured paths are bounded to 4096 encoded bytes and 128 components. Permanent ownership lock files are never replaced. Separate noninheritable file descriptions own nonblocking flock leases; lifetime ownership lasts through actual closure. The order is work admission, stage/deletion ownership, optional address stripe, then the short root transaction. No transaction or root gate spans payload I/O, callbacks, readers or waiting for work.

SQLite is private provider bookkeeping, separate from the metadata database. All connections, including reads, serialize under the nonblocking root gate and close before file effects. Configuration is DELETE journal, FULL synchronization, MEMORY temporary store, zero mmap, a 256 KiB page cache, zero busy timeout and FULL auto-vacuum from creation. Busy/locked state returns bounded pending without internal retry. Durable intent precedes file effects; flushed effects precede durable outcomes. Recovery establishes namespace durability even when an earlier link/directory is already present or an earlier removal is already absent; observation alone does not flush that effect. Recovery processes one occupied slot per call. Actual database/journal allocation and settled growth are measured separately from logical payload credits.

The finite data-job allowance includes individual reader pulls as well as mutations and replay verification. A returned reader retains its stream ticket and shared target ownership between pulls, without retaining a data-work ticket while its consumer is idle. Each pull obtains ordinary data-work admission before creating its owned task. Shutdown closes those streams and joins their actual read jobs together; failure of one read does not abandon other cleanup. Cancellation never drops a native future or releases its target ownership before actual completion, including repeated cancellation.

| Setting | Default and supported range |
|---|---|
| Private payload bytes | 2 GiB; 1 MiB–16 GiB |
| Stage slots | 8; 1–32 |
| Deletion slots | 8; 1–32 |
| Root data-work slots | 4; 1–8 |
| Root reader slots | 8; 1–16 |
| Root control-work slots | 2, fixed |
| Per-receiver queued/running data or verifier jobs | 8, fixed |
| Per-receiver open stream handles | 8, fixed |
| Per-receiver queued/running control jobs | 2, fixed |
| Executor threads | 4 payload and 2 control per receiver |
| Address ownership stripes | 64 permanent locks |
| Stream chunk | At most 64 KiB |
| Inventory query/page | At most 128 rows; complete serialized DTO at most 16 KiB |

One data mutation owns a stage at a time. Nonwaiting local admission precedes task/job/descriptor allocation. Cancellation retains admission and ownership until actual executor finalization. Streams retain explicit reader and stage/address leases through actual close. `aclose` seals admission, closes owned streams and joins strongly owned jobs before releasing lifetime ownership; repeated calls share one completion operation. An arbitrary blocked filesystem syscall may leave close unresolved, never falsely completed. Private capacity reserves the actual simultaneous peak: raw bytes N plus max(N, prepared cap C), with bounded file/block/control overhead measured separately. An inode with several private/content links is counted once but remains owned until its final link/reader closes.

Storage DTO version is a required strict integer 1. Collections are immutable tuples, all nested records are closed, and each complete DTO fits 16 KiB/depth 16. A compact manifest stores per-part declarations and immutable part identities under one StageRef, rather than repeating StageRefs. Operation request digests use UTF-8 canonical JSON with sorted keys, compact separators and unescaped Unicode: `{version: 1, kind, storage_identity, operation_id, arguments}`. All optional fields appear explicitly as null, and every nested version and target/manifest/preparation field is included. The operation's `request_digest` is excluded. A receiver validates this binding before effects. Fixed vectors and changed-field tests establish the recipe. Digests retain the `sha256:` prefix.

Inventory is a keyset query over immutable byte-ordered keys. Only the ordered prefix fitting the full page envelope is emitted; the continuation key is the last emitted key, never a discarded fetched row. Saved cursor identity/kind must match the receiver and requested scan. Inventory grants no ownership or closure authority. Diagnostics contain only safe operation, outcome, reason, counts and duration, never payload digests, private references/paths or arbitrary backend exceptions. Local implementation and executable evidence are tracked in [the implementation review record](../reviews/local-artifact-storage-2026-10-23.md).

## 8. Artifact type (FR-4.1–4.3)

```python
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class BuiltinArtifactTypeBinding:
    name: str
    schema: JsonObject
    parent: str | None
    revision_descriptor: JsonObject

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class ArtifactTypeCapabilities:
    pass

class ArtifactType(Protocol):
    name: str
    parent: str | None
    config_schema: JsonObject

    @property
    def schema(self) -> JsonObject: ...

    def capabilities(self) -> ArtifactTypeCapabilities: ...
    def validate(
        self, payload: JsonValue, ctx: ProtocolContext
    ) -> list[ContractViolation]: ...
    def digest_fields(
        self, payload: JsonValue, ctx: ProtocolContext
    ) -> list[str]: ...
    def summary(self, payload: JsonValue, ctx: ProtocolContext) -> JsonValue: ...

class VcsRefArtifactType:
    def __init__(
        self, configuration: JsonObject, binding: BuiltinArtifactTypeBinding
    ) -> None: ...

def create_provider(
    configuration: JsonObject, binding: BuiltinArtifactTypeBinding
) -> VcsRefArtifactType: ...
```

The installed provider name and the bound artifact name have separate roles. `artifact_type.vcs_ref` has metadata name `vcs_ref`; its checked instance has artifact name `VcsRef`. Its deployment configuration is the closed empty object, independently of its saved artifact schema. `ArtifactTypeCapabilities` is a frozen value with no flags. The three payload hooks are synchronous computation and receive the caller-bound context for diagnostics; none performs I/O or acquires an external resource.

`BuiltinArtifactTypeBinding` is an operation-local constructor-input record of the artifact name, expanded saved schema, parent and complete immutable revision descriptor. Its frozen dataclass prevents field reassignment but does not freeze nested JSON containers. The VcsRef constructor validates those supplied values and retains its own detached checked schema/descriptor snapshot; its schema property returns a defensive view. Later mutation of input containers or returned views cannot change the instance. The binding is not the authoring `BuiltinTypeContract` declaration, a new persisted execution-contract format or authorization evidence. Before payload work the constructor requires `VcsRef`, null parent, the complete supported descriptor and strict parsed equality with the exact packaged revision-1 schema, ignoring only object member order. The descriptor's schema hash refers to the published file bytes; a matching claimed hash cannot replace checking the supplied schema. Trusted composition owns retrieval from the authorized saved graph-version contract. No current registry alias, network resolver or persisted executable is consulted.

Compatibility admission precedes copying, serialization and general parsing of supplied schema/descriptor structures. Exact container/scalar types and cardinality are checked before key membership and values; traversal follows only the trusted fixed contract's keys and indexes. Cardinality mismatches do not enumerate the mismatched container, and unexpected deep values are not recursively explored. Bool/int and int/float substitutions, unknown keywords and changed annotations refuse. The fixed-shape descriptor preflight guards the existing revision helper before it can enumerate supplied keys. After exact equality, the provider retains only a snapshot of the verified fixed contract; rejected input is never copied or serialized. These fixed-revision checks introduce no general schema input quota.

For VcsRef, `validate` returns an empty list or the existing single content-free `ContractViolation` from the model boundary. `digest_fields` and `summary` also validate their payload and refuse invalid values rather than returning extraction results. The provider uses non-retryable `artifact_type_binding_incompatible`, `artifact_type_configuration_invalid` and `artifact_type_payload_invalid` errors with fixed messages. Details contain only a fixed rule identifier or the existing fixed VcsRef violation code. No native exception text, submitted name, schema or payload content enters the error or its chaining. An ordinary returned validation violation is not an operational exception; unexpected failures retain caller-owned error reporting.

The payload hooks accept admitted `JsonValue`. They do not decode raw bytes or recover token evidence discarded by an earlier decoder. Untrusted VcsRef raw ingress continues to use `parse_vcs_ref_json`; API canonical-wire validation and production authority checks remain separate. A model-backed provider pass does not establish those consumer guarantees.

The installed CommandResult implementation is selected as artifact_type.command_result, with metadata name command_result and logical name CommandResult. It uses the same closed empty deployment configuration, empty frozen capabilities, operation-local BuiltinArtifactTypeBinding and per-call synchronous context ABI. Binding requires the exact packaged CommandResult revision-1 schema, null parent and complete published descriptor, including canonicalization and every validation parameter. The fixed-structure comparison shared with VcsRef preserves exact JSON kinds, cardinality, keys and values before copying supplied data; retained snapshots derive only from verified trusted values.

CommandResult validate delegates to parse_command_result and returns zero or one existing safe CR violation. Digest selection is a fresh [""], and summary is the complete detached model identity. Invalid extraction/configuration/binding uses the existing nonretryable artifact_type_* error contract with fixed messages and only safe rule/violation details. Raw decoding remains parse_command_result_json. The provider never fetches a capture reference: full supplied-byte integrity for captures larger than the retained tail remains verify_command_result_captures and its real consumers. Model-valid descriptors establish neither captured-byte provenance/ownership nor command success, current authority, closure or runtime activation.

The [built-in revision catalogue](artifacts/README.md) publishes VcsRef revision 1: closed schema, `kora.vcs-ref-validation.v1`, complete-payload digest selection and identity summary. Its byte/depth/path and strict-decoding predicates supplement generic JSON Schema validation; shape validity does not establish publication provenance. Whole-payload selection is stored as `[""]`, the root JSON Pointer. Built-ins ship as immutable schema/behavior revisions, expanded into the deployed contract (ADR-0018). A bound `ArtifactType` instance takes schema/parent and exact behavior identities from the saved graph-version contract, not the current registry alias. Custom types are registered through the API (`PUT /artifact-types`) as schema, parent and declarative behavior descriptors, with an optional trusted installed entry-point binding for custom `summary`. Registry writes update the authoring alias, never any deployed closure. Registration checks JSON Schema draft 2020-12 and the supported closed-reference rules in [ir.md](ir.md) section 2.1; deployment uses embedded definitions, not replacement aliases.

The generic `validation` identity is `kora.json-schema-2020-12.v1`; `kora.json-payload.v1` selects the whole canonical payload for digesting and returns the payload as the generic identity summary. A custom identity pins provider-declared semantics and the trusted installation's immutable implementation binding. It is not executable persisted data or an automatic package download. A reused identity with mismatched implementation metadata is incompatible; required semantics must be supported before the affected validation/execution operation. Compatible upgrades retain bindings required by active runs.

[StructuredOutput revision 1](artifacts/StructuredOutput.md) admits a direct `JsonValue` under `kora.structured-output-validation.v1` and `kora.structured-json.v1`; its schema is in the saved effective contract, never a payload wrapper. It selects `[""]` and returns a detached identity summary, including available root null. `ContractViolation` is the shared content-free diagnostic record, not a native JSON Schema/Pydantic exception. The StructuredOutput behavior returns at most one fixed safe violation; arbitrary payload keys and schema-authored names cannot become diagnostic paths or messages. The generic signatures allow every JSON root while each bound contract enforces its own narrower domain; VcsRef remains object-only. The VcsRef implementation activates the common ArtifactType ABI and its VcsRef provider conformance under work item 8. Complete StructuredOutput effective-schema evaluation and effective-output integration conformance remain pending their implementing delivery.

The installed standalone provider is selected as `artifact_type.structured_output`, with metadata name `structured_output` and logical name `StructuredOutput`. Configuration is the closed empty object. Binding requires the exact packaged revision-1 schema, null parent and complete immutable descriptor before retaining trusted detached snapshots. Validation delegates to `parse_structured_output`; digest selection is a fresh `[""]`, and summary is the complete detached identity value, including root null. Invalid extraction/configuration/binding follows the existing content-free nonretryable ArtifactType error contract. Raw decoding remains `parse_structured_output_json`. This exact built-in binding carries no inline schema or custom parent/reference closure, so it cannot establish complete effective-output validity or activate producer/API/history/retention consumers.

[ADR-0027](../../docs/decisions/0027-bounded-effective-schema-validation.md) assigns future complete effective validation to a pure `kora.protocols.effective_artifact_type` composition over trusted bound behaviors and the pure `kora.ir` preparation/arithmetic/evaluator. No hook, constructor ABI, provider registration or package-import permission changes. The helper uses the same owned direct value for inherited model admission, every required behavior and applicable saved parent/declared/inline constraint; closure membership alone is not another assertion. Inherited refusal cannot be waived. Affected generic `kora.json-schema-2020-12.v1` behavior obtains exact schema truth from the prepared evaluator, or a trusted binding implementing the same immutable exact semantics; ordinary stock numerical validation cannot falsely refuse first. Non-schema custom behavior remains required, and unknown/mismatched behavior bindings refuse before evaluation.

The [StructuredOutput execution design](artifacts/StructuredOutput.md#3-saved-effective-schemas-and-cel-view) owns detached original/target identity, safe execution views, support exclusions, non-boolean refusal propagation and resources. The complete resource, numerical, handler and adapter schedule is owner-approved under [#515](https://github.com/realkarych/kora/issues/515#issuecomment-6533958110). Its `kora.structured-output-schema-policy.v1` identity remains reserved/unavailable for effective execution until recursive equality, every supported bounded handler, safe pinned-engine adaptation, complete evaluation and common composition meet their named implementation and verification gates. The internal numerical foundation supplies no complete schema truth, artifact-acceptance value or extraction authority. Future runner, API and scheduler consumers use this same composition and exact saved policy input; only `valid` supplies an owned acceptance value for effective digest/summary extraction. `invalid` and `refused` retain the distinct [fixed result contract](diagnostics.md#structuredoutput-effective-results); internal errors retain caller-owned reporting. Provider discovery/authorization and saved-record authenticity remain the consumer/deployment owner's responsibility. No evaluator/consumer activation, list aggregate support, hostile-provider containment or wall-time guarantee is supplied here.

[AgentSession revision 1](artifacts/AgentSession.md) supplies a JsonValue logical validation/digest view while its primary stored bytes remain the opaque transcript. Its custom validation binds metadata, denial sample and immutable transcript descriptor; its full logical digest selection and fixed safe-metadata summary are distinct from physical transcript identity. Core constructs the frozen `kora.agent-session-view.v1` view before CEL path cutting, rather than projecting arbitrary logical-value fields. The standalone installed provider under [#485](https://github.com/realkarych/kora/issues/485) implements logical validation, full logical selection and the fixed safe summary through the existing ArtifactType ABI. Transfer envelopes, transcript-byte ownership and saved CEL projection remain separate consumer contracts; provider validity grants no permission to read a caller-selected storage ref. Derived `List[AgentSession]`, recursive lists and scheduler collect/export aggregate materialization/persistence and command/public-read delivery remain unavailable until work items 3/9/10, coordinated with 11/12 and actual transcript/sample owners 17/#216, approve and save the finite full-aggregate canonical-byte/depth/item-count/admission semantics and satisfy the [aggregate activation prerequisite](artifacts/AgentSession.md#derived-list-aggregate-activation). Admission precedes full allocation. Per-result, upload and CEL bounds are insufficient; no aggregate numerical default or campaign is introduced. Work items 3/4/8/9/10/11/12/15/17 and #216 must supply their remaining named producer, replay, access, saved CEL projection and retention evidence before consumer activation.

The installed provider is selected as `artifact_type.agent_session`, with metadata name `agent_session` and logical name `AgentSession`. Configuration is the closed empty object. Its constructor binds the exact packaged revision-1 schema, null parent and complete immutable descriptor, including representation, logical identity and checker-policy compatibility, before retaining trusted detached snapshots. The existing fixed-structure comparator preserves exact JSON kinds and cardinality before copying supplied data. Validation delegates to `parse_agent_session`; invalid extraction uses the existing fixed nonretryable ArtifactType errors without parser exception chaining. `digest_fields` returns a fresh `[""]` over the complete logical value, while `summary` delegates to `summarize_agent_session` and excludes transcript/denial and hidden observation fields. Raw admission remains `parse_agent_session_json`; actual supplied transcript integrity remains `verify_agent_session_transcript`. No hook performs transcript I/O or establishes observation/pricing authority, physical byte receipt, source provenance, authorization, replay or consumer activation.

Here `JsonObject`/`JsonValue` describe JSON shapes, not automatic use of the bounded `JsonObjectField` of sections 2/3/6/12. Saved schema objects retain the IR embedded-schema domain and independent schema-work admission; each payload/summary uses its bound artifact contract, including separately saved list aggregate semantics. The annotation alone cannot impose protocol-field caps on a schema, a different artifact type or an enclosing list.

Runner/harness selection keeps the existing outer optional `SessionResult.output` and inner `OutputCandidate.value: JsonValue`. An absent candidate or harness null sentinel is not a produced null. Existing allowed strict-text selection can produce the value `null`; unsupported root capabilities refuse explicitly. Preservation begins at the admitted typed snapshot, including scheduler admission from retained event/observation/approval sources; it cannot recover earlier normalized tokens. Work items 6/8/17 own the corresponding producer/provider parity and absence-versus-null conformance.

At production/upload, store the applied contract digest, validation outcome, actual digest-field selection and returned summary/projection. User-content diagnostics and projection values follow payload retention even if small/inline; content-free outcome/selection/digests remain metadata. Built-in content-bound approval projections are computed once under the saved contract and reused, never recomputed with a replacement plugin. Historical reads/diffs load these saved results and explicit availability; they do not call today's `summary`, `digest_fields` or validator. Branches, fan-out collection and approvals share these contracts/results despite having no physical runtime attempt. CEL expressions read neither `summary` nor any other type behavior: kora core cuts their projections from the validated payload under the graph version's frozen path sets ([ir.md section 7.5](ir.md#75-projections-and-typed-conversion)), separately from approval digest projections. Required expired payloads or unavailable behavior cause explicit failure, not current-value fallback.

[CommandResult revision 1](artifacts/CommandResult.md) pins `kora.command-result-validation.v1`, `kora.command-result-json.v1`, whole-root selection `[""]` and a content-bearing identity summary. Its root is canonical execution evidence, with independent stdout/stderr descriptors and exact byte tails after the existing StreamMasker incremental UTF-8 replacement decode, literal masking and UTF-8 re-encode; parsed `output_file` JSON remains a separate declared artifact. The 1 MiB raw/canonical root, depth-16, 64 MiB per-stream captured-prefix and 4 KiB retained-tail limits are type-specific parameters. The root is not server-truncated. Shape validation does not establish trusted checkout/protection/capture facts or termination. The current closed worker upload envelope has no command stdout/stderr selector: work item 12 with 11/13/14/17 and #216 must approve and implement the capture wire/owner mapping and its lifecycle gates before real consumer activation. Derived List[CommandResult], recursively derived lists and scheduler collect/export aggregates remain pending work items 3/9/10/11/12 under the generic saved list semantics; require approved aggregate admission/materialization, persistence and delivery before activation. Per-result, upload and CEL bounds do not supply aggregate bounds; no new aggregate numerical default or campaign is selected. This publication introduces no new route, output role or direct worker storage authority.

## 9. Observability exporter (FR-11.3, NFR-9.2)

This exporter consumes durable domain events, independently of the [component diagnostic stream](observability.md#1-streams-and-authority). Its own failures use bounded diagnostics and independent counters; they are not recursively exported as new domain events. Diagnostic level filtering or overflow cannot drop durable event rows.

```python
class ObservabilityExporter(Protocol):
    name: str
    config_schema: dict
    async def on_event(self, event: EventRow, ctx: ExporterContext) -> None: ...   # every row of the append-only event table, in order
    async def flush(self) -> None: ...
```

One event concept (current contract [protocols](protocols.md)): exporters are loaded in the control plane only and consume the `event` table, so an export can be **replayed** from any point by re-reading rows; the scheduler feeds them through a bounded queue with drop-with-metric and an exporter never blocks a transition. Session facts reach the table because the API promotes them on upload: `vcs.*` from the worker's publish results and `permission_denied` from the runner's segments become rows. The built-in `otel` exporter follows the GenAI semantic conventions (current contract [protocols](protocols.md)): a run is an `invoke_workflow` span with `gen_ai.workflow.name = <graph id>`; on `attempt.finishing` it reads the attempt's session segments through `ctx.segments(attempt_id)` and builds the `invoke_agent` span, `CLIENT` kind when the harness runs remotely or managed, `INTERNAL` otherwise, with `gen_ai.usage.*` from the usage records; `gen_ai.conversation.id` is the harness session id, so kora spans join with Claude Code's own OpenTelemetry output on `session.id`. Langfuse and Datadog exporters follow the same interface.

## 10. Authentication (FR-12.4, NFR-4.4)

Standalone defaults and client credential selection follow [ADR-0011](../../docs/decisions/0011-namespaces-and-personal-tokens.md) and [the bootstrap contract](scheduler.md#10-standalone-sqlite-versus-postgres): two personal tokens, one owner, no new token kind or purpose flag. The authenticator returns each configured principal's actual flags/capabilities; the MCP server adds no permission model. The env secrets provider's `KORA_*` exclusion belongs to #141 and is implemented by #192.

The shared types in `kora.protocols.authentication` are implemented under [#506](https://github.com/realkarych/kora/issues/506). `Principal` is a closed frozen validated value with `token_id`, `kind`, `owner`, a tuple of unique `NamespaceGrant(namespace_id, transcripts)` entries, exact boolean `approve` and `platform`, and nullable `runtime`. A user has no runtime; a service requires its runtime and cannot carry personal flags or transcript rights. Empty namespace grants add no authority. Input namespace arrays are detached into frozen entries, and validating an existing model revalidates its nested fields rather than trusting construction or unchecked copies. `RequestInfo` holds the raw nullable Authorization header, excludes it from its string representation and defaults `requires_current_authority` to false. This value is internal: trusted caller code supplies the freshness requirement. Concrete `authenticate` and `revoke` methods receive `AuthenticationContext` for the caller-bound logger and clock; `capabilities()` returns the frozen `AuthenticationCapabilities`. These shared types do not implement token validation, operation authorization, revocation storage or provider activation.

`AuthenticationContext` is a frozen value with exactly `logger: ComponentLogger` and `clock: Clock`. Both `Authenticator.authenticate` and `revoke` receive it. It carries no namespace, run, attempt, secret resolver/admission, request digest, deadline or activity reporter and grants no authority. Callers do not fabricate or infer a namespace to construct it. Authentication implementations and their direct/installed conformance callbacks accept this context; other provider families continue to use `ProtocolContext`. Existing caller-authorized logger scope is preserved, but pre-authentication API ingress must not invent or inherit unauthorized namespace identity. The existing expiry, revocation and freshness rules are unchanged. The public context change is approved under [issue #534](https://github.com/realkarych/kora/issues/534); external authentication implementations adopt the new type without a compatibility cast.

```python
class Authenticator(Protocol):
    name: str
    config_schema: JsonObject
    def capabilities(self) -> AuthenticationCapabilities: ...
    async def authenticate(self, request: RequestInfo, ctx: AuthenticationContext) -> Principal: ...
    async def revoke(self, token_id: str, ctx: AuthenticationContext) -> None: ...
```

`RequestInfo.requires_current_authority` is an internal boolean, default false, set only by trusted operation code. The API sets it true for follow-stream record authorization and the final metrics check. True requires an authoritative validation that bypasses cached prior authority or proves relevant revocation/grant/capability changes invalidate it; it never grants rights. A provider unable to meet it raises `KoraProtocolError(authentication_unavailable, fixed safe text, True, {})` and returns no principal. Ordinary API and bounded-wait checks retain their existing at-most-one-second nonlocal-provider cache tolerance. `authenticate` returns a validated `Principal` under that requested freshness requirement or raises `KoraProtocolError` with code `authentication_rejected`, fixed content-free text, `retryable=False` and empty details for missing, malformed, unknown, expired or revoked credentials. The API maps only this expected refusal to API009 without copying provider text or details. `authentication_unavailable` and other recognized transient dependency failures map to API011; unexpected provider failure maps to API010. Neither returns an anonymous principal or grants authority. The API error registry, current-authority rules and installation-metrics grant are defined in [API conventions](api-and-agent-surface.md#error-codes-and-statuses). Authentication conformance exercises expected refusal, recognized unavailability, unexpected failure and credential freshness without leaking validity categories at any diagnostic level.

Two principal kinds (current contract [ADR-0011](../../docs/decisions/0011-namespaces-and-personal-tokens.md), DD-32): a **user** principal is a personal token bound to a person and a namespace list, with exactly two flags, `approve` (may respond to approvals) and `platform` (may publish presets, namespace policies and tool policies), and a per-namespace `transcripts` capability for reading transcripts and session segments (current contract [protocols](protocols.md)); no roles or groups. A **service** principal belongs to a worker, is bound to a namespace list and may register, claim attempts of its runtime in those namespaces, heartbeat, upload, transition and perform bounded worker-only reads for attempts whose current grant it holds (checked by worker, incarnation, domain, dispatcher and lease epoch). Every by-id route joins on `namespace_id` against the principal's list (current contract [ADR-0011](../../docs/decisions/0011-namespaces-and-personal-tokens.md)). Built-in: `static_token` (MVP). OIDC implements the same `Principal` so routes do not change. There are no tokens for agent nodes: nodes never call kora (DD-5).

### Static-token provider (issue #506)

The installed entry point is `authentication.static_token`. Metadata discovery imports no database/server modules; database imports begin only at provider construction/use. Its factory takes a closed empty configuration and an existing `Database` borrowed from the composition root. The provider creates no engine, background task or credential cache and never closes the borrowed database. Each authentication performs one indexed unique-digest read. The HTTP Bearer scheme is case-insensitive, with one or more ASCII spaces before its nonempty ASCII bearer value; credential bytes are hashed exactly with SHA-256, without case folding, stripping or decoding. No plaintext credential or new token kind is persisted. Issuance and credential entropy remain the bootstrap/token-management owner's responsibility.

The provider uses the existing `kora.db.reads.token_by_digest`/`TokenRow` boundary and releases the database unit before validating the detached authority and checking expiry. `kora.db.tokens` owns the atomic token update and database-driver failure classification; the provider imports no raw driver or table handle. Both ordinary and current-authority calls read current committed rights without a cache. Expected credential invalidity has the same `authentication_rejected` error, `Authentication is required.` message, false retryability and empty details. Recognized unavailable storage has `authentication_unavailable`, `Authentication is unavailable.`, true retryability and empty details. Corrupt authority or an unexpected database failure has nonretryable `authentication_failed`, `Authentication failed.` and empty details. No dependency or validation exception is retained as a cause/context. Cancellation propagates and the existing database lease owner completes connection release. API error conversion, operation-specific authorization and the final protected-content submission point remain with the API implementation.

The owner accepted permanent revocation through the existing expiry field on 2026-10-30 under [#506](https://github.com/realkarych/kora/issues/506#issuecomment-6531702175), with [acceptance recorded](https://github.com/realkarych/kora/issues/506#issuecomment-6532244535). The normalized UTC instant `1970-01-01T00:00:00Z` is reserved as revoked state. Every static-token read refuses that exact value independently of its clock, before applying the ordinary `now >= expires_at` predicate to other non-null expiries. A row already carrying the reserved value is revoked. Ordinary natural expiry retains its trusted-clock semantics; it does not become sticky expiry or establish a shared non-regressing clock.

`revoke` atomically sets the sentinel where the token ID matches and expiry is null or differs from the sentinel, committing before success. Unknown IDs and repeated/concurrent revocations are successful no-ops. Rows, token identities and worker/idempotency/history references remain intact. This preserves monotonic authority rather than monotonically decreasing timestamps: replacing an earlier expiry may increase its numeric value but cannot restore authority. The previous expiry and revocation time are not retained. Issuers and future management code must reserve the sentinel and issue a new credential/identity rather than renew a revoked row. This delivery adds no issuance or recovery command, schema migration, HTTP operation or separate permission model.

`kora.conformance.authentication` supplies shared cases for exact principals, uniform refusals, committed authority changes, permanent revoke, safe unexpected failure and unavailable authentication without stale fallback, together with common capability/configuration/failure/logging checks. The typed `AuthenticationConformanceInputs` uses caller-owned setup/change/unavailability operations and the exact selected provider instance. Authentication checks and its failure/configuration callbacks receive `AuthenticationContext`; direct and installed adapters construct that value from their owned logger/clock before invoking the selected implementation, including the common failure, logging and configuration checks. The conformance driver may retain its own `ProtocolContext` internally, but does not pass it as an authentication context. Direct injected pytest cases are synchronous and use the standard-library `kora_authentication_runner` fixture; resource-owning provider/inputs fixtures use that same runner for asynchronous setup and cleanup. No asyncio pytest plugin is required. The installed command runs the same check functions in its existing owned loop. Provider integration adds expiry thresholds, pre-epoch/backward clocks, concurrent revoke, retained references, real connection failures, malformed stored authority and raw stored-value decoding, cancellation and safe diagnostics. These provider results do not activate API or standalone consumer gates.

## 11. Sensor (FR-2.3, FR-6.2, DD-2)

A sensor observes an external condition on behalf of a `wait_event` node or a sensor trigger. It is polled by the scheduler at the configured interval; it holds no worker and keeps no state between polls beyond what it returns.

```python
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class SensorCapabilities:
    supports_push: bool             # can also deliver observations as inbound events (webhook), not only by poll
    min_interval: timedelta         # the scheduler refuses shorter intervals; protects code hosts from tight polling (DD-37)
    needs_vcs: bool                 # built-ins change_request_status and ci_status

class Sensor(Protocol):
    name: str
    config_schema: dict             # JSON Schema for `poll.config`; validated at kora validate (IR003-style error with YAML path)
    def capabilities(self) -> SensorCapabilities: ...
    async def observe(self, config: dict, inputs: dict, ctx: ProtocolContext) -> Observation: ...
        # returns a JSON object exposed to the `until` CEL expression as `observation`; sensors are stateless between polls
```

Built-ins: `change_request_status` and `ci_status` (through the VCS protocol; observation is the `ChangeRequestStatus` or `CiStatus` DTO), `http` (GET a URL with optional headers from secret references; observation is `{status, body}`; the URL must match the namespace's `http_allowlist` and link-local and cloud-metadata addresses are always refused, current contract [protocols](protocols.md)). Adopters register sensors through the `kora.provider` entry point and reference them by name in `poll.sensor`.

## 12. Policy (DD-29)

`Policy.validate` returns `ContractViolation`, an alias of the [shared `Diagnostic`](diagnostics.md). Paths are relative to the checked policy, and the caller owns document rebasing and error severity. Invalid policy remains `IR013`; unsupported namespace coverage remains `IR019`, while unsupported node-only security controls are refused with `IR028` under the [first-slice contract](first-slice.md#refusal-contract). The shared per-call count/byte limits are enforced before appending; overflow raises `KoraProtocolError` with code `diagnostics_limit`, fixed safe text and no payload details. Incomplete provider validation cannot authorize deployment or become warning-only success.

The policy protocol is the **decision point for tool calls**. The session runner invokes it from the harness's `PreToolUse` hook before every tool call the harness routes through that hook, the harness's output channel excepted (below); a deny stops the call and travels as a `permission_denied` session event that lands on the `AgentSession` artifact and is promoted to an event row on upload (current contract [ADR-0009](../../docs/decisions/0009-target-repository-trust-boundary.md)). Approval requests are not the decision point: the Codex app-server raises `item/commandExecution/requestApproval` and `item/fileChange/requestApproval` only when the agent needs to leave its sandbox, never for a command or an edit inside `workspace-write`, and `item/permissions/requestApproval` belongs to its `request_permissions` tool ([harness policy hooks](../research/harness-policy-hooks.md), checked 2026-10-16; [ADR-0009](../../docs/decisions/0009-target-repository-trust-boundary.md)).

**Mechanism per adapter.** `claude`: the Agent SDK `PreToolUse` callback registered without a matcher, the only decision point: no `allowed_tools` or `disallowed_tools` list is derived from the policy, because a harness rule cannot express argv-level indirection or protected paths and a harness deny would override the per-layer `allow_deny` evaluation. The harness runs the callback before every tool call it does not reject itself, in every permission mode per its documentation, and deny decisions hold under `bypassPermissions`. Calls the harness rejects itself never reach the callback: a permission rule's denial appears in `ResultMessage.permission_denials` and becomes a `permission_denied` event with `source: harness`, while a call that a built-in tool's own input validation rejects never runs and is an ordinary failed tool result, not a denial (section 3); a call without a callback decision that is neither is a policy bypass that fails the attempt. The [probe of 2026-10-19](../research/claude-agent-sdk-probe-2026-10-19.md) observed this order for a settings deny rule and the Bash tool's own validation, and observed denies under `bypassPermissions` only, of shell and file-edit calls and of a tool on an in-process SDK MCP server, a kind the adapter does not use; stdio and HTTP MCP servers, the other permission modes, a callback allow under `dontAsk`, subagent calls and hosted tools rest on the [documentation](../research/claude-code-documented-controls-2026-10-19.md) until the scripted-model conformance cases below run. Under `trust_repo_config: true` the repository's configuration is trusted code outside the policy: per the Claude Code documentation its hooks run in parallel with the callback and can return `updatedInput`, which replaces the input after the decision; a project `disableAllHooks` setting may switch off the callback itself (not yet observed); its settings can change the harness's endpoint, proxy or TLS trust; and Claude Code reloads project settings the agent writes during the session, so the model can extend that configuration. Namespace denies are therefore not guaranteed under `true`, while protected paths are still checked at gate time; the namespace accepts this residual with `allow_trust_repo_config` ([ADR-0009](../../docs/decisions/0009-target-repository-trust-boundary.md), amended 2026-10-19). In both modes Claude Code's built-in plugins, whose list the adapter checks against a reviewed set, may carry hooks of their own. `codex`: a `PreToolUse` command hook, `kora-runner policy-hook`, that reads the hook input from stdin, obtains the decision from the runner and exits 0 to allow or 2 to deny with the reason on stderr; the runner declares it as a managed hook in the attempt's `requirements.toml` with `allow_managed_hooks_only = true`, so the hook is trusted by policy and the repository's own hooks never load; `approval_policy` stays `never` and plays no part in the policy. `acp` (after MVP): `session/request_permission`, which an agent may or may not raise; that adapter declares its coverage per agent and ships only once verified.

**Coverage matrix.** Each adapter declares in `HarnessCapabilities.policy_coverage` (section 3) which call kinds its hook intercepts. The shared installation-aware validator exclusively compares every node and namespace `tool_policy` pattern with this typed coverage and emits hard errors (`IR028`, `IR019`, [ir.md](ir.md) section 10). It also checks requested protections against typed capabilities and the trusted implementation inventory before collecting adapter warnings. `check_config` returns only non-security warnings and neither decides policy coverage nor converts a required protection into a warning. For the MVP adapters, under every kora `permission_mode` and `sandbox` value, as the Claude Code and Codex documentation and the Codex sources stated on 2026-10-16; for `claude`, the probe of 2026-10-19 observed the shell and file-edit rows, and the MCP row for an in-process SDK server only, under `bypassPermissions`:

| call kind | pattern examples | `claude` | `codex` |
|---|---|---|---|
| shell command | `Bash`, `Bash(etl *)` | intercepted (`Bash`) | intercepted (`Bash`) |
| file edit | `Edit`, `Write` | intercepted (`Edit`, `Write`, `MultiEdit` and `NotebookEdit`, the tool names pending confirmation under work item 15) | intercepted (`apply_patch`; the hook matcher accepts `Edit` and `Write` as aliases) |
| MCP tool call | `mcp__<server>__<tool>` | intercepted | intercepted |
| tool call inside a subagent | any pattern | intercepted; the hook input carries `agent_id` | intercepted; subagent hooks carry the parent session id |
| web and other hosted tools | `WebSearch`, `WebFetch` | intercepted (built-in tools) | **not interceptable**: Codex web search is a hosted tool outside its hooks; the adapter sets `web_search = disabled` in the attempt configuration and the runtime network policy is the layer that holds |
| harness-internal actions (project instructions, skills, memory) | none | not a tool call; governed by `trust_repo_config` through `setting_sources` and, for MCP server configuration, `strict_mcp_config`; auto-memory is off in both modes (`CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_AUTO_MEMORY`, per-attempt config directory) | not a tool call; governed by `trust_repo_config` through `project_doc_max_bytes` and `allow_managed_hooks_only` |

**Three layers.** The hook is one of three layers and the only one that sees tool names and arguments: (1) runtime isolation bounds the process regardless of what the agent calls (filesystem, network policy, credentials, process rights; `RuntimeCapabilities.enforces_network_policy`, DD-21); (2) the permissions of external services bound what a call can do (the rights of the tokens a session holds, the capabilities an MCP server exposes, publication through the worker rather than through the session); (3) the harness hook denies a call before it runs. A policy is the set of rights these layers enforce; a tool-name or argv check does not prove the absence of a forbidden effect inside an allowed program, so the shell-chaining rule below complements the first two layers and never replaces them.

**Fail closed.** A `tool_policy` pattern that names an uncovered call kind is error `IR019` when it comes from the namespace policy, because the platform team's rule would otherwise run unenforced, and error `IR028` when it comes from the node alone.

```python
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class ToolCallRequest:
    harness: str
    tool: str                       # harness tool name, e.g. "Bash", "Edit", "mcp__yt__query"
    tool_use_id: str
    agent_id: str | None
    arguments: JsonObject           # repr=False; runner-local, never journalled
    workspace: WorkspaceRef
    node_path: str                  # at most 1,024 printable ASCII characters, no space

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class ToolPolicy:
    allow: tuple[str, ...]
    deny: tuple[str, ...]
    extensions: JsonObject          # the `x-` fields other implementations read

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class PolicyLayers:
    namespace: ToolPolicy
    node: ToolPolicy

PolicyReason = Literal["allowed", "denied", "protected_path", "not_allowed", "request_invalid", "argument_invalid"]

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class PolicyDecision:
    allow: bool                     # exactly a bool
    reason: str                     # a rule id or a PolicyReason, never echoed arguments

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class PolicyCapabilities:
    pass

class Policy(Protocol):
    name: str
    config_schema: JsonObject       # schema of the `tool_policy` object nodes carry
    def capabilities(self) -> PolicyCapabilities: ...
    def validate(self, policy: JsonObject) -> list[ContractViolation]: ...   # kora validate, IR013
    async def decide(self, request: ToolCallRequest, layers: PolicyLayers, protected_paths: tuple[str, ...], ctx: ProtocolContext) -> PolicyDecision: ...
```

One policy applies to every call (current contract [ADR-0009](../../docs/decisions/0009-target-repository-trust-boundary.md), amended 2026-10-21): the node's `tool_policy` and the namespace's `tool_policy` (set by the platform team, DD-32 `platform` flag) are evaluated **per layer**, and `decide` receives both layers, not a merged value. A call is allowed only when no `deny` pattern of either layer matches it and every layer with a non-empty `allow` has a matching pattern, so a namespace deny always wins and a node never widens a namespace allow list. An empty `allow` adds no restriction; a layer with only denies relies on its deny patterns. Order inside an array has no effect. `allow_deny` ignores `tool_use_id` and `agent_id`, so decisions stay deterministic for identical requests; `agent_id` is read only by the adapter's output-channel exception (section 3). The one exception is the harness's own output channel: on `claude` the adapter allows the synthetic `StructuredOutput` tool without consulting the policy, only under the closed exceptions of section 3 (exact name, `output_format` passed, main session, and never after `max_events`), without evaluating either layer. An exception or timeout in `decide` denies the call and fails the attempt with cause `policy_error` or `policy_timeout` (section 3). `IR013`, checked by kora's own validator for every policy implementation and wherever a `tool_policy` is validated, the namespace's included, rejects a pattern whose tool name is exactly `StructuredOutput`; a wildcard pattern stays valid and never applies to the output channel (ADR-0009, amended 2026-10-19). The MVP implementation `allow_deny` matches the allow and deny patterns of each layer in kora's own grammar, under the rules of "The `allow_deny` policy (issue #233)" below. A shell call with a form on that subsection's closed indirection list matches every governing `Bash` deny of either layer, and no allow pattern ever matches it, so it is denied whenever a layer with a non-empty `allow` applies (current contract [ADR-0009](../../docs/decisions/0009-target-repository-trust-boundary.md), amended 2026-10-23). Argv matching is the hook layer, not proof that an effect is absent (three layers above). It denies every file-edit tool on `protected_paths` whether or not an allow matches (DD-34). Cedar and OPA arrive as plugins through the same interface, reading their own `x-` fields per layer, without an IR change. The policy runs inside the runner, so the decision is local to the session and never requires the agent to reach kora.

**Decision reasons (issue #236).** A `PolicyDecision.reason` is either a rule id or a `PolicyReason`, so no argument text can reach a decision:

- **A rule id** has the form `<layer>.<allow|deny>.<index>`: `<layer>` is `namespace` or `node`, and `<index>` is the pattern's zero-based position in that layer's array, written as `0` or a decimal without a leading zero below 10,000, as in `namespace.deny.3`. Pattern text never appears. A provider that cannot cite a rule by position, including one whose array is longer, uses `denied` for a deny and `allowed` for an allow.
- **A `PolicyReason`** is one of a closed set: `allowed`, `denied`, `protected_path`, `not_allowed`, `request_invalid` and `argument_invalid`. A provider with other refusals maps them onto it.
- **The reason agrees with the decision.** `allow` is true exactly when the reason is `allowed` or an allow rule id.
- **A decision becomes a `permission_denied` event** (section 3) like this: a deny rule id gives `pattern: <rule id>` and `code: denied`; any other refusal gives `pattern: None` and the reason as the `code`; `allowed` never reaches an event. A harness denial carries no `pattern`.

`ToolCallRequest` and `PolicyDecision` check their fields when constructed, with fixed messages: exact `str` identifier fields, an exact `str` `node_path`, a parsed bounded `arguments` object, an exact `bool` for `allow` and the reason form above. A request the check refuses is a denied call, never an allowed one: the runner denies it with `request_invalid` and fails the attempt with cause `policy_error`; it emits the `permission_denied` event only when the call's `tool` and `tool_use_id` are valid event fields, and otherwise counts the denial without one, because the refusal names only the first field it refused. A `decide` that raises, times out or returns anything other than an exact `PolicyDecision` fails the attempt with cause `policy_error` or `policy_timeout` and emits `permission_denied` with `source: harness`, `pattern: null` and that cause as its code. These two codes are records the runner writes on the supervisor side (section 2); section 3 files them under `source: harness` because `PolicyReason` has no failure codes. `arguments` is a plain parsed `dict`, so the runner builds a fresh `ToolCallRequest` for each `decide` and never reads it back afterwards. `Policy.capabilities` returns an empty `PolicyCapabilities`, as section 1 requires of every protocol.

The static grammar foundation under [issue #518](https://github.com/realkarych/kora/issues/518) is shared through `kora.ir.tool_patterns`. The provider consumes the same compiler and grammar-only refusal enumeration; document parsing, violation representation/limits, runtime matching, harness tool table, fuel and cache remain provider-owned. Kora's `tool_policy_refusals` additionally checks the existing universal exact `StructuredOutput` rule; `validate_tool_policies` applies it to declared agents. The pure core checker is reusable by a future namespace consumer, but namespace provisioning and installation-aware coverage remain unimplemented by this pass. Existing array bytes remain the sum of raw pattern UTF-8 with the compiler's existing surrogatepass encoding, not JSON punctuation. No provider ABI, matching or capacity change is introduced.

### The `allow_deny` policy (issue #233)

**Patterns.** A pattern is `Name` or `Name(argument)` with a non-empty argument, at most 4,096 characters; an `allow` or `deny` array holds at most 128 patterns and 64 KiB, and one layer holds at most 8,192 wildcard pieces (each pattern counts its `*` plus one) and 16,384 characters outside ASCII, refused as `IR013`; the protected globs of a call hold at most 8,192 pieces (each glob counts its `*` and `/` plus one), 64 KiB and 16,384 characters outside ASCII together, refused as `protected_paths_invalid`. Case folding costs per character, so these bounds, not fuel, cap the compile cost. So a configuration compiles within the cold ceiling.

- `Name` uses `[A-Za-z0-9_.*-]`, with `*` as a wildcard; `mcp__<server>` alone matches every tool of that server; this assumes server names contain no `__`, which validation of the IR is to refuse (work item 3).
- The argument runs from the first `(` to the final `)`, which ends the pattern, and contains no `(`, `)` or control character. `*` in it matches any run of characters, `/` included; consecutive `*` collapse.
- Every piece the matcher searches for (every piece but the last, after collapsing) is at most 96 characters, in the exact and the case-folded form, so each search stays linear; a longer one is refused.
- A deny pattern in which a `*` touches a character of nonzero combining class in the decomposed or the case-folded form, at the end of the piece before it or the start of the piece after it, is refused: canonical reordering can move another combining mark of the call's text across the wildcard, so the deny would miss a spelling of the name it denies. Allow patterns compare exact code points and keep such wildcards.
- A pattern governs a call when its name matches the tool name. Its argument then applies to the tool's extracted argument, or to each edited path of a file-edit tool. A wildcard-name pattern's argument may match nothing for some tools it governs: `E*(/abs/x)` governs the file-edit family but can never match an edited path.
- **The file-edit family.** A deny whose name matches `Edit` or `Write` (such as `Edit`, `E*` or `*`), and an allow named exactly `Edit` or `Write`, govern every file-edit tool of the harness: `Edit`, `Write`, `MultiEdit` and `NotebookEdit` on `claude`, `apply_patch` on `codex`. A pattern naming one tool governs only that tool. Keeping the table complete is pending work item 15: the adapter is to compare the built-in tool list each session reports with the reviewed set for its version and fail `config_error` on a difference. `MultiEdit` is listed from the Claude Code tool names, pending that confirmation.
- **Edit-path arguments.** In a pattern whose name, with no wildcard, is `Edit`, `Write` or a file-edit tool, the argument is a root-relative path pattern: an absolute argument, a leading `~`, and an empty, `.` or `..` segment are refused. Unlike a protected path, `Edit(tests)` does not cover `tests/a.py`.
- **Differences from the harnesses.** `:*` anywhere in a pattern and an argument starting with `//` or `~/` are refused rather than reinterpreted; arguments are globs, not gitignore patterns; a deny on `Edit` or `Write` also governs the other file-edit tools.
- **An argument pattern on a tool with no extraction rule** (`Read(src/*)`, `mcp__fs__read(x)`) fails closed: as a deny it matches, as an allow it never matches.

**`validate`** returns each refusal as `IR013` with the pattern's JSON path, such as `["deny", 3]`, and a fixed message per kind: a malformed pattern, a pattern, array or searched piece over its bound, a document that is not a tool policy, an unsupported harness form, a refused edit-path argument, and a deny wildcard that touches a combining mark; a layer over its piece or non-ASCII bound is refused once, with no path. It stops at the diagnostics bound with `diagnostics_limit`.

**Arguments.** Only the named field is read, and only when a rule needs it: for `Bash`, under the shell condition, when an allow governs `Bash`, or when a governing deny has an argument; for a file-edit tool, under the path condition. Other keys are ignored; a missing or non-string field is `argument_invalid` whenever it is read, and when no rule needs it a governing deny decides by name.

| harness and tool | argument |
| --- | --- |
| `claude` `Bash` | `command` |
| `claude` `Edit`, `Write`, `MultiEdit` | `file_path` |
| `claude` `NotebookEdit` | `notebook_path` |
| `codex` `Bash` | `command` |
| `codex` `apply_patch` | `command`, the patch text |

The `codex` shapes are read from its source ([Codex hook tool input](../research/codex-hook-tool-input-2026-10-23.md)): the hook input carries neither the command's working directory nor its shell, and `write_stdin` emits no hook, so input written to a running process is outside the policy on `codex` until work item 15 disables or bounds it. The shell rule below assumes the command runs under an sh-family shell; pinning or refusing the model-chosen `shell` and `login` on `codex` and fixing `SHELL` for `claude` are pending work item 15. `allow_deny` accepts the harnesses `claude` and `codex`; the fake harness sends the harness the IR names. Any other harness raises `policy_harness_unsupported`.

**Two conditions.**

- **The path condition**, for a file-edit call: protected paths are set, or a layer has a pattern governing the tool. Every path rule below applies only when it holds; otherwise the paths are left to the runtime layer.
- **The shell condition**, for a `Bash` call: protected paths are set, or a layer has a pattern governing any file-edit tool of the harness (a deny by the deny rule, an allow by the allow rule), single-tool patterns included. Under it, a command that mentions `apply_patch` or `applypatch` anywhere (case-folded), or that cannot be scanned in full, is `argument_invalid`: Codex applies such a shell-routed patch itself after the `Bash` hook, with a working directory the hook does not carry. The detection is best-effort; shell writes stay with the gate-time `changed_paths` check of section 4.

**Paths.** An empty path, a path with a control character, a leading `~`, leading or trailing white space (the JavaScript `trim` set), text UTF-8 cannot encode, or more than 4,096 characters is `argument_invalid`. On `claude` the hook receives the harness-expanded absolute path ([harness edit-path resolution, 2026-10-24](../research/harness-edit-path-resolution-2026-10-24.md)); confirming that for each tested version belongs to work item 15. A path whose kernel walk differs from the kernel walk of its lexical form, as when a `..` follows a symbolic link, is `argument_invalid` too, because a harness that collapses `..` before opening the file would write a file neither form names. A relative path is joined to `WorkspaceRef.root`, the session's working directory as the harness sees it, which must be absolute with no empty, `.` or `..` segment, and is resolved as the kernel resolves it.

- An iterative resolver walks the raw path with `lstat` and `readlink` as the kernel does: from the resolved root when the path starts with a spelling of the root, otherwise from `/`. A missing component ends the walk and the rest is appended lexically, so new files can be created; a `..` after a missing component, more than 40 links, and any other error are `argument_invalid`.
- The lexical form is taken relative to whichever of the lexical and resolved roots prefixes it (the longer), and the resolved form relative to the resolved root. Each must lie under a root, or the call is `argument_invalid`.
- Denies and protected paths compare in canonical caseless form (NFD, case folded, then NFD again, so a glob prefix compares in decomposed form and also covers a name whose next character carries a combining mark), with the interpreter's Unicode case tables, and match if either form matches; allows compare exact code points and match only if both forms match.
- An edit of an existing regular file with more than one hard link is `argument_invalid`.
- `decide` assumes the candidate's path namespace and a local filesystem. Effects outside the checkout, including a write through a link swapped after the check, belong to the runtime layer, and the per-attempt harness directories to the adapter.

**`apply_patch`.** A patch is at most 64 KiB and is parsed as Codex's envelope: lines split only at `\n` with one trailing `\r` removed, as Rust's `str::lines` does; marker and header lines containing a control character or a line or paragraph separator refused, and a `Move to:` line with surrounding white space refused, because Codex keeps that white space in the target; trimmed headers, the heredoc-wrapped form, `*** Begin Patch` and `*** End Patch`, `Add File:`, `Update File:`, `Delete File:` and `Move to:`. A line that trims to text starting with `***` is a marker line; unclassified marker lines, text after the end marker, `*** Environment ID:` and a patch with no path are `argument_invalid`. This also refuses an indented `***` line, a recognised header included, that Codex would read as update context, which fails closed. A deny or protected path matches if any path matches; an allow passes only when every path matches some allow of that layer.

**The shell rule.** A linear scanner reads the command once, tracking quotes, and splits it into words with quote removal. Indirection is any of:

- a command longer than 16,384 characters or with an unterminated quote (these cannot be scanned in full), or an empty command;
- a control character, newline or carriage return; outside quotes any of `` ; & | ( ) < > ` $ \ { } # * ? [ ] ~ ! ^ ``; inside double quotes any of `` $ ` \ ! ``; inside single quotes a backtick; and `(` or `{` right after a `$` of the same word however each is quoted, because builtins that evaluate an argument as an array subscript or a math expression run the command substitution it holds (zsh `exit 'path[$(…)]'`), and a `$` that closes a single-quoted span still joins the `(` that follows it in double quotes;
- in the first word: a quote, a non-ASCII character, a leading `-`, or a `NAME=value` or `NAME+=value` assignment;
- a first word whose case-folded basename is a shell (`sh`, `bash`, `zsh`, `dash`, `ksh`, `fish`, `csh`, `tcsh`, `ash`, `mksh`, with an optional `r` prefix and version suffix, such as `rbash` or `zsh-5.9`), a builtin that evaluates an argument such as an array subscript (`let`, `declare`, `typeset`, `local`, `export`, `readonly`, `read`, `printf`, `print`, `unset`, `test`, `[`, `mapfile`, `readarray`, `wait`, `getopts`, `integer`, `float`, `vared`, `exit`, `return`, `shift`, `break`, `continue`, `logout`, `bye`, `compgen`, `complete`, `ulimit`, `jobs`), a wrapper or launcher (`env`, `xargs`, `eval`, `exec`, `source`, `.`, `command`, `builtin`, `sudo`, `doas`, `nohup`, `timeout`, `nice`, `time`, `stdbuf`, `setsid`, `busybox`, `awk` with an optional `g`, `m` or `n` prefix, `osascript`, `uv`, `uvx`, `npx`, `bunx`, `pnpm`, `poetry`, `watch`, `caffeinate`, `arch`, `script`, `flock`, `chroot`, `ionice`, `taskset`, `chrt`, `unbuffer`, `parallel`, `su`, `runuser`, `strace`, `ltrace`, `gdb`, `ssh`, `tmux`, `screen`, `nix-shell`), `apply_patch` or `applypatch`, a reserved word or modifier (`!`, `coproc`, `trap`, `noglob`, `nocorrect`, `if`, `while`, `until`, `for`, `case`, `select`, `function`, `then`, `do`, `[[`, `repeat`, `foreach`), or starts with `python`, `pypy`, `perl`, `ruby`, `node`, `bun`, `deno`, `php`, `lua`, `tclsh` or `pwsh`;
- `find` with `-exec`, `-execdir`, `-ok` or `-okdir`; and anywhere `-c`, `-e`, `--exec`, `--eval`, `--exec=`, `--eval=`, `-E`, a short-option cluster of two or more letters ending in `c`, `e` or `E` (`bash -lc`, `perl -le`), or a word starting with `=` (zsh expands it to a command's path). So `grep -e` and `grep -ie` can never be allowed.

The canonical form is the words joined by single spaces. A `Bash(argument)` deny matches when the argument matches the case-folded canonical form, with empty quoted words dropped so `rm '' -rf x` still reads `rm -rf x`, from its start, or from just after any space, `/` or `=`, so it also catches `foo rm -rf x`, `/bin/rm -rf x` and `rg --pre=rm -rf x`; a deny argument ending in a space followed by `*` also matches the command without that tail. With indirection every governing `Bash` deny matches. An allow compares exact code points from the start of the canonical form and requires no indirection, a first word without `/` and no word containing whitespace; the trailing space-and-`*` rule applies to denies only, and no allow pattern of any form matches a call with indirection.

**The guarantee and its limits.** A deny cannot be bypassed through the listed forms; the list is closed (ADR-0009, amended 2026-10-23 and 2026-10-24). Global options before a subcommand, scripts, build tools, and programs outside the list that run an argument as a command can still reach a denied effect (`git -C . push` against `Bash(git push *)`, so write `Bash(git *push*)`; an operand before the options, as in `rm junk -rf x` against `Bash(rm -rf *)`, so write `Bash(rm *-rf*)`; a launcher or evaluation option the list does not name, such as `fish --command=` or `php -r` behind an unnamed launcher), and an allowed command can still run code the agent wrote: shell start-up files, a directory on `PATH` inside the workspace, `.git/config`, build files. Hook-time protected paths cover the file-edit tools only; shell writes and MCP file tools are caught by the gate-time check of section 4. These effects belong to the runtime and external-permission layers (three layers above).

**Order and reasons.** `decide` first raises, in order, `policy_harness_unsupported`, `tool_policy_invalid` (a layer `validate` would refuse) and `protected_paths_invalid` (an invalid glob, or a list past 64 globs, 8,192 pieces, 64 KiB or 16,384 characters outside ASCII); each is a denied call with cause `policy_error`. It then evaluates paths, the shell refusals, protected paths, namespace then node denies by index, and namespace then node allows, stopping at the first outcome:

| order | condition | reason |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | an argument, patch or path that cannot be read, a shell refusal under the shell condition, or exhausted fuel | `argument_invalid` |
| 2 | a protected path | `protected_path` |
| 3 | a matching deny | its rule id, such as `namespace.deny.3` |
| 4 | a layer with a non-empty allow and no matching pattern | `not_allowed` |
| 5 | otherwise | `allowed` |

**Fuel.** `decide` never awaits, so `policy_timeout` cannot interrupt it. Every decision instead draws on one fuel counter, `kora.ir.globs.Fuel`, charged before each core match (a fixed charge per call, per edge piece and, more, per middle piece, plus the first and last pieces' lengths, then the window between the first and last piece weighted by the longest middle piece, charged only once the first and last pieces match and middle pieces remain; a literal match pays its text's length instead), each anchor search (one window weighted by its first piece as a core needle, because a search for a short needle costs up to the needle's length per character, plus a call charge for every occurrence of the first piece that does not follow a separator), each glob match (a fixed call charge) and each glob position, each `lstat` or `readlink` and each path at intake (more for a path outside ASCII); exhaustion denies the call with `argument_invalid`, never allows it. The `test_policy_decision_cost` rows of `tests/measurements/test_protocol_models_cost.py` calibrate the budget and report, as a measurement gate rather than an assertion, whether the worst decision stays below 100 ms; an ordinary decision spends at most a third of the budget, as the realistic rows show for a 4 KiB command and a 64-file patch under 100 patterns per array and 10 protected globs; a realistic 16 KiB command is decided without exhausting the fuel. Those claims hold for that envelope of short pattern prefixes: a configuration at the pattern caps whose deny prefixes are long may exhaust the fuel on a long command and fail closed. A patch whose paths times patterns exceed the budget is refused as `argument_invalid`; that realistic configuration admits about 200 files. Each row asserts its declared outcome, so a row refused early fails. Parsing the layers and compiling the protected globs, the command scanner and the patch parser are bounded by the caps above instead. A provider keeps the parsed configurations of its four most recently used layer and protected-path sets, so a configuration in use pays for parsing only on its first decision; with more than four configurations in rotation every decision pays. The rows report that cold decision as its own measurement gate: at the caps it costs several times a warm decision, because parsing is bounded by the caps rather than by fuel. A compiled pattern keeps only the form its kind compares: an allow its exact form, a deny its case-folded form and tail-free copy. At the caps, with four distinct arrays of mixed-width pieces, one cached configuration holds about 4 MB together with the strings its key keeps, at most 5 MiB for each entry by assertion, released with the provider. A decision is deterministic for an identical request, layers and protected paths on a filesystem unchanged along every walked path.

**Records.** `policy.allow_deny.allowed` (DEBUG, `reason`), `policy.allow_deny.denied` (INFO, `reason`, and `cause`: `rule` or `fuel`) and `policy.allow_deny.refused` (WARNING, `code`) carry no argument, path, pattern or tool name.

## 13. Conformance suites (NFR-10.2)

Each protocol ships `kora.conformance.<protocol>`, cases marked `kora_conformance` that an adopter imports into a test module. The cases take the provider under test from fixtures of the opt-in plugin `kora.conformance.plugin` (`kora_<protocol>_provider`, `kora_<protocol>_exercise`, for secrets `kora_secrets_resolvable`, `kora_secrets_missing_reference`, `kora_secrets_forbidden_reference` and `kora_secrets_listed_prefix`, and for the policy `kora_policy_calls`), which raise until the adopter overrides them; entry-point command integration and an isolated built-and-installed third-party fixture are required by first-slice readiness under [#215](https://github.com/realkarych/kora/issues/215); the injected foundation does not claim that evidence. The plugin imports only pytest, and `kora.conformance` needs the `kora[conformance]` extra (issue #204).
